rates. They therefore hold simultaneously at the tie.

4.4    Products of correlated parameter differences
At λ = 1 the forms B and D coincide on Γ. Accordingly the preceding scalar tests alone do not
separate their rates. We first prove the parameter fact needed for this case.

Lemma 4.6 (Product coefficients). Let ν be a law of pairs d = (rd , td ) in a bounded planar set.
Assume its time marginal is Frostman s, its tilt marginal is Frostman θ for some θ > 0, and
0 < s ≤ 1. Assume also that its simultaneous two-coordinate refinements have an affine hereditary
profile of dimension at least s: in typical intermediate cells the normalized unrestricted increments
satisfy the Frostman s bounds at all subsequent fixed block subresolutions. For a pin f = (rf , tf )
define
                                      Qf (d) = (rd − rf )(td − tf ).
The following alternatives suffice at every fixed finite grid of resolutions.
  (i) If some strip of width m−χ , with fixed χ > 0, has dense ν mass, restrict ν to that strip. At any
      fixed resolution m−h with 0 < h < χ sufficiently small, the coefficients Qf (d) have Frostman
      exponent s, up to subpower loss, on dense pairs away from the time diagonal.
 (ii) If every strip of each fixed power width has power-small mass, then for every σ < s and every
      fixed h ∈ (0, 1], pairs lying in coefficient bins of conditional mass greater than m−hσ have
      power-small total mass. After removal of these exceptions on a sufficiently fine finite grid,
      the pinned coefficient laws satisfy Frostman bounds with exponent arbitrarily close to s and
      arbitrarily small power loss.




                                                   46
Proof. The dense-strip alternative. Suppose first that a strip of width m−χ has dense mass. Its
slope a = dr/dt satisfies
                                   m−o(1) ≤ |a| ≤ mo(1) .
Indeed, if |a| ≤ m−ε along a subsequence, its intersection with the bounded parameter region lies
in a tilt interval of length O(m−ε + m−χ ). The positive tilt Frostman exponent makes its mass
power-small. If |a| ≥ mε , including the vertical case, use the time marginal instead. Since this
applies to every fixed ε > 0, it proves the claim. Conditioning on the strip costs only a subpower
factor in either marginal bound.
    On the strip write
                                                        p
                         r = at + b + e,     |e| ≤ m−χ 1 + a2 = m−χ+o(1) .

Uniformly in its pins and targets,

                                 Qf (d) = a(td − tf )2 + O(m−χ+o(1) ).

The time Frostman bound permits removal of pairs with |td −tf | < m−o(1) , with the cutoff decreasing
sufficiently slowly that the lost mass is negligible compared with every dense incidence being retained.
On each sign branch of td − tf the quadratic has derivative of magnitude between m−o(1) and mo(1) .
At radii ρ ≥ m−h , with fixed h < χ, the inverse image of a coefficient interval is therefore contained
in at most two time intervals of length mo(1) ρ. The time marginal bound proves (i), also at all the
intermediate radii needed for a projection test.

Power-light strips and the conditional query law. For (ii), the planar law is Frostman s,
by the simultaneous refinement hypothesis. Lemma 4.3 therefore applies. Fix σ < s and h > 0.
Suppose that pairs landing in bins of Qf of conditional mass at least m−hσ carry dense incidence E.
Condition the product law on this incidence. For each pin the conditioned output occupies at most
O(mhσ ) bins, so, with Ph the output bin,

                                     H(Ph | f ) ≤ hσ log m + O(1).                                (4.5)

All entropies in the rest of this proof are under the conditioned law unless explicitly described as
unrestricted.
    Choose σ < s′ < s and divide [0, h] into J equal blocks of length η = h/J. The integer J will be
large but fixed. Let Dj be the target parameter cell at depth jη, and let Pj be the nested bin of Qf
at that depth. For j ≥ 3 we claim that, apart from a negligible conditioned mass,

                            H(Pj+1 | f, Dj ) ≥ (s′ − ε)η log m − o(log m),                        (4.6)

where ε > 0 is arbitrarily small. We verify carefully the conditioning and direction estimates behind
this assertion.
    Use the hereditary parent estimates on this fixed block grid under the original target law ν,
before conditioning on E. Choose their errors and parent-trim thresholds as in Lemma 3.2, so that
the omitted original cell mass is negligible compared with the dense incidence being retained. For a
remaining value C of Dj , let c be its center and let µC be the normalized original restriction ν|C ,
expressed in the cell’s increment units. This unrestricted law is Frostman s at all the required block
subresolutions. The following density trims compare the conditioned query and target fibers to
this law. The map ψC (v) = c + m−jη v returns an increment to its physical target cell. Define the
pulled-back incidence and its target fibers by

                   EC = {(f, v) : (f, ψC (v)) ∈ E},      EC,f = {v : (f, v) ∈ EC }.

                                                  47
The pin law ν remains in the original parameter coordinates. Set

                                           αC = (ν × µC )(EC ).

After discarding cells with too small an incidence density, αC = m−o(1) in cells carrying almost all
of the conditioned mass. To see this, the total incidence in cells with αC < ρ is at most ρ; choose
the subpower threshold ρ much smaller than the original dense incidence. The query law in such a
cell is
                                         µC (EC,f )
                              qC (df ) =            ν(df ) ≤ mo(1) ν(df ).                     (4.7)
                                            αC
Another density trim ensures µC (EC,f ) = m−o(1) for almost all contributing queries. The same
reasoning applies after any of the finitely many required power-small exceptional pair sets has been
removed.

Radial control of the query directions. The gradient of Qf at c is

                                       ∇Qf (c) = (tc − tf , rc − rf ).

Thus its direction is the radial direction from f to c with the coordinates interchanged. Remove
the exceptional pairs from Lemma 4.3, in both orders and at a fine grid of angular powers, using
exponent s′ , for the physical pairs (f, ψC (v)). This removes a negligible fraction of the dense
incidence. Direction estimates may be transferred from an actual surviving target d ∈ C to the
center c. For comparison pins at distance at least m−η from c, this changes the direction by

                                     O(m−jη /m−η ) = O(m−(j−1)η ),

which is below the block angular mesh for j ≥ 3. Comparison pins closer than O(m−η ) have mass
at most m−ηs+o(1) by planar Frostman. The surviving queried pin itself can be required to be at
distance at least m−o(1) from the target, so its direction also changes by less than the block mesh.
For a ball centered at a surviving query direction, choose its corresponding good target in C and
use its radial estimate. Enlarging the angular ball by the above errors bounds all the far comparison
pins; the near ones have just been bounded separately. Re-centering balls extends the conclusion
to every angular interval. In view of (4.7), the law of gradient directions under qC is consequently
Frostman s′ , with any prescribed small power loss down to the block mesh. The grids and prior
losses are chosen sufficiently fine and small for that purpose.

Collision bounds inside a target cell. Here is the elementary projection-energy calculation in
the normalized cell. Let v, v ′ be independent with law µC , and let ω have the gradient-direction
law. A projection collision at mesh m−η requires ω to lie in boundedly many angular intervals of
total length O(m−η /|v − v ′ |). Therefore its averaged probability is bounded by
                                ZZ
                                                                 ′
                          mε0        min{1, (m−η /|v − v ′ |)s } dµC (v)dµC (v ′ )
                                                             ′
                                                    ≤ m−ηs +ε1 .                                 (4.8)

The last estimate follows by summing annuli and using the Frostman s bound, with s > s′ . Both
ε0 , ε1 can be chosen arbitrarily small compared with η. Markov’s inequality gives this collision
bound, with another arbitrarily small loss, for almost all queries. Restriction to EC,f multiplies the
collision probability by at most µC (EC,f )−2 = mo(1) . Thus the estimate applies to the target fibers
actually present in the conditioned incidence, rather than just to unrestricted increments.

                                                     48
   The gradient magnitude is at least m−o(1) after the near-diagonal removal. Normalizing it
changes the mesh to m−η+o(1) , an admissible subpower change in (4.8). Taylor expansion gives, for
bounded v,
                    Qf (c + m−jη v) = Qf (c) + m−jη ∇Qf (c) · v + m−2jη vr vt .
The remainder is below the physical next resolution m−(j+1)η . Hence a bin of the nonlinear output
is covered by boundedly many bins of the linearized output at the relevant normalized mesh. Finally,
for bin probabilities ai ,           X                    X
                                  −     ai log ai ≥ − log    a2i .
                                        i                       i

The collision estimate yields (4.6). The negligible removed conditioned mass is restored using
entropy concavity. All bounds are averaged with respect to the actual conditional query laws, so no
independence after conditioning has been assumed.

Summing the output entropy increments. Because Qf is uniformly Lipschitz on the bounded
parameter region, Dj together with f determines Pj up to bounded ambiguity. Nested output bins
also give

                     H(Pj+1 | f ) − H(Pj | f ) ≥ H(Pj+1 | f, Dj ) − H(Pj | f, Dj ).               (4.9)

Indeed the left side is H(Pj+1 | f, Pj ), and conditioning further on Dj can only decrease it. The
subtracted right-hand term is O(1). Sum (4.9) for j = 3, . . . , J − 1 and use (4.6). This gives

                           H(Ph | f ) ≥ (J − 3)(s′ − ε)η log m − o(log m).

Choose J so large, and then ε and all other losses so small, that (1 − 3/J)(s′ − ε) > σ. This
contradicts (4.5).
    We have ruled out a subpower sequence of bad pair masses, and therefore proved a power-small
bound at each fixed coefficient scale. Remove these exceptions on a finite fine grid. The conditional
mass bound at the bin containing any surviving coefficient gives the centered Frostman bound there;
an interval meeting the retained support is covered by a bounded enlargement centered at one of
its points. Normalizing dense pin fibers and interpolating between grid powers costs an arbitrarily
small power. This proves (ii).

4.5   The tied spatial pair
We now return to the coincident spatial forms. The middle pair (B, D) remains one joint measurement.
Overlaps of full spatial fibers will produce scalar projections with coefficients supplied by Lemma 4.6;
no separate rates for the two tied coordinates are assumed.

Proposition 4.7 (The equal-slope tie). Suppose λ = 1 and θ > 0 in (4.4). At a generic point of Γ
there is a tangent with singleton spatial rates p, g, an affine diagonal rate H for the pair (B, D),
and an affine diagonal parameter rate. These rates satisfy

                                      g ≥ fs (p),        H ≥ fs (p).                             (4.10)

Proof. On Γ the coordinate forms are C, C + Y twice, C + 2Y , and Y twice. Peeling separates the
singleton groups C, A and the paired groups (B, D) and (Y, R); each group has an affine restriction
to its diagonal. This gives the claimed form. Write J(B, D) for the separated middle-pair summand
of this actual tangent. On lower-side full alignment, R < Y is equivalent to B < D. Further

                                                    49
full-alignment tangents there preserve p and have time rate at least s, so the scalar time test gives
∂D J ≥ fs (p) almost everywhere in B < D. For h > 0, monotonicity in B and integration of this
D-bound give

                   Hh = J(b + h, b + h) − J(b, b) ≥ J(b, b + h) − J(b, b) ≥ hfs (p).

The integration first holds on almost every vertical segment; continuity includes every such segment
and its boundary endpoint. This proves the inequality for H without assigning separate B, D
partials at the tie. If p = 0, both assertions in (4.10) are immediate. Assume from now on that
p > 0.
    Take a block in a typical fully aligned parent on the tie. In normalized units write the spatial
entries in its starting frame as x, y, z, w, and fine parameter pairs as d = (rd , td ) with law ν. The
parameter diagonal rate is at least s. Heredity makes its law Frostman of that diagonal dimension,
also inside intermediate parameter cells at every later fixed subresolution. The time marginal is
Frostman s, and the tilt marginal has exponent at least θ > 0, by (4.4) and the conditional-parameter
lower bound. Thus the parameter laws satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma 4.6.
    Denote the parent by z0 and the block length by ℓ > 0. With ei denoting the coordinate vectors,
set
                                  zsp = z0 + ℓ(eC + eB + eD + eA ),
                                 zpar = z0 + ℓ(eY + eR ),
                                zjoint = zpar + ℓ(eC + eB + eD + eA ).
The spatial increment of zsp has exponent p + H + g. Group additivity gives the same exponent for
zjoint conditional on zpar . The marginal-weight comparison in the proof of Proposition 4.4, with
spatial exponent p + H + g and the parameter diagonal exponent, therefore gives dense incidence
under the product of the actual spatial and parameter marginals. Given the fine parameter label d,
write the spatial entries in its frame as x, yd , zd , wd . The two further states

                           zpar + ℓ(eC + eA ),      zpar + ℓ(eB + eD + eA )

have increments ℓ(p + g) and ℓ(H + g) from zpar . The remaining refinement from the first of these
states to zjoint costs ℓH. All these states satisfy (3.2); in particular the fine parameters provide the
slack for these partial spatial refinements.
    Lemma 3.1 rebases the full spatial labels using the known fine parameters, with bounded
ambiguity. Lemmas 3.2 and 3.6, applied to these state pairs at every required block subresolution,
make the conditional (x, wd ) law planar Frostman p + g. Its additional (yd , zd ) fibers have support
upper exponent H, and the output triples (yd , zd , wd ) have support upper exponent H + g. We use
these conditional laws before any overlap restriction.
    We spell out how the dense spatial overlaps carry the weights needed for Lemma 4.2. This also
ensures that subsequent choices of parameters do not replace a weighted projection problem by a
mere cardinality assertion. Let m′ be the base of the positive-length block presently used; it may
be a fixed fractional block. In a typical parent, let Sf be the coarse-frame spatial bins incident to
a fine parameter bin f . Coarse-frame and fine-frame full spatial bins determine one another up
to bounded coverings when f is given. The exact truncated parameters are used in subtracting
center-dependent translations, so large absolute centers create no new error.
    Put L = p + H + g. The common spatial universe is the support of the spatial label at zsp , of
size at most (m′ )L+o(1) . For almost every tested f , Sf has size (m′ )L+o(1) , and every retained joint
cell has pre-trim conditional weight at least (m′ )−L−o(1) given f . These conclusions follow from
the support and maximal-weight bounds for zjoint conditional on zpar , followed by discarding cells

                                                   50
below the reciprocal-support threshold. The lower weights refer to this conditional law before that
final discard. The exceptional fraction can be made negligible compared with any prescribed dense
parameter restriction, by taking the threshold slack to zero sufficiently slowly. In particular the
entire sets Sf remain available after such a restriction.
    For any dense restriction of the parameter law, Cauchy–Schwarz in the common spatial universe
gives
                      ZZ                              X Z                     2
                           |Sf ∩ Sd | dν(f )dν(d) =           1Sf (x) dν(f )
                                                      x
                                                     ( |Sf | dν(f ))2
                                                          R
                                                 ≥                    ≥ (m′ )L−o(1) .
                                                   |spatial universe|

Here x in the sum denotes a full spatial cell, not only its first coordinate. Since the overlap upper
bound is (m′ )L+o(1) , a dense set of parameter pairs has overlaps of full exponent L. Power-small
exceptional parameter pairs can be removed before selecting these overlaps.
   For one such pair f, d, put ∆r = rd − rf and ∆t = td − tf . The shear update gives, to mesh
accuracy,

                                    yd = yf − ∆r x,                            zd = zf + ∆t x,
                 wd − ∆t yd + ∆r zd = wf + ∆t∆r x.                                                   (4.11)

The last identity follows by substituting the first two into the full shear update, including its mixed
term. Bounded bin errors produce only bounded mesh errors in normalized units.
    There are at most (m′ )p+g+o(1) (x, wf ) bins in the overlap. Discard those carrying fewer than
(m′ )H−o(1) additional full cells, with the slack chosen sufficiently slowly. They account for a negligible
fraction of an overlap of exponent L. Call the remaining input bins rich. Inside one rich input
bin, the different full cells map to different triples (yd , zd , wd ) up to bounded multiplicity: once x is
fixed to mesh accuracy, the frame change can be inverted from that triple. Thus a scalar value on
the right of (4.11), attained in one rich input bin, uses at least (m′ )H−o(1) output triples. Scalar
values separated by a sufficiently large mesh constant use disjoint triples, because the left side of
that identity is determined to mesh accuracy by a triple. The total output support has size at most
(m′ )H+g+o(1) . Consequently the scalar projection

                                        (x, wf ) 7−→ wf + ∆r∆t x

of the rich subset has at most (m′ )g+o(1) bins.
    These rich subsets are dense for the original conditional (x, wf ) law. Indeed they retain (m′ )L−o(1)
joint cells, each with pre-trim conditional weight at least (m′ )−L−o(1) . Their total weight is therefore
(m′ )−o(1) , and their image consists of the corresponding coarse-accuracy input bins. The planar
Frostman bound is the one before this final restriction, obtained from the matched C, A advances.
The rich restriction costs only a subpower factor. This is the required passage from overlap counts
to weighted point fibers.
    We now obtain enough coefficient directions. If ν has dense mass in a strip of width m−χ for
some fixed χ > 0, first restrict the parameters to this strip and repeat the overlap argument with
the full spatial sets of the typical parameter bins. Use a fixed positive fractional block ending at
depth h < χ sufficiently small. Lemma 4.6(i) gives a Frostman s law of ∆r∆t on dense pairs. The
removed time-diagonal pairs have negligible mass compared with the dense overlaps. All spatial
rate, fiber, and weight statements above apply at this fractional depth with m′ = mh .


                                                      51
    If there is no such strip restriction along the realizing sequence, pass to a subsequence on which
every strip of each fixed power width has power-small mass. This dichotomy is obtained by inspecting
the maximum strip mass on countably many fixed power grids; bounded mesh enlargements suffice.
Lemma 4.6(ii) gives coefficient exponents arbitrarily close to s off power-small exceptional pairs.
Remove these pairs on the finite fine grid of coefficient resolutions before choosing a pin with dense
overlap.
    In either case, averaging selects f with densely many targets d that have the coefficient Frostman
bounds and the rich projected-input subsets just constructed. Parameter binning changes the
coefficient only by mesh order. If several target bins produce the same coefficient bin, choose one
corresponding rich input subset for that bin; each has the required dense mass for the same pre-trim
conditional (x, wf ) law. Thus the coefficient marginal together with these rich subsets supplies dense
product incidence for Lemma 4.2. The map from a bounded scalar coefficient to the associated
projection direction is bi-Lipschitz in a bounded chart.
    Applying that lemma and letting all coefficient and restriction losses tend to zero yields

                                  g ≥ min{p + g, (p + g + s)/2, 1}.

Since p > 0, either g = 1, or the first and third terms exceed g and the middle term gives g ≥ p + s.
In both cases g ≥ fs (p), completing the proof.


5    Excluding calibrated aspect paths
We complete the proof of Theorem 2.2. Suppose, to the contrary, that

                                            q∗ < 1 + 2s − 10κ.                                           (5.1)

The preceding construction supplies a calibrated entropy profile and finite parameters

                                   a ≥ 0,        b ≤ 1,     λ = a − b.

We may take further simultaneous tangents at generic points of the calibrated line. The support
inequality, its equality on that line, the conditional parameter profiles, and the predictability
statement survive these operations by Lemmas 3.4 and 3.6. All actual entropy profiles below have
been regularized as in Lemma 3.2. Thus a “dense” restriction means a restriction of subpower
relative mass, and support exponents used in a conditional block have the corresponding typical-bin
weight bounds. These conventions also apply to iterated tangents.
    The cases below exhaust the finite parameters supplied by the calibration. The table records
their roles; every comparison is proved in the corresponding subsection.

 Aspect                    Further distinction                  Comparison mechanism
 λ=0                       a > 0 or a = 0                       Isotropic profile, radial test, and strip-axis
                                                                reduction.
 λ > 0, a > 0              θ = 0; or θ > 0 with λ ̸= 1 or       Longitudinal comparisons, pure-order costs,
                           λ=1                                  and the joint-coordinate tie.
 λ > 0, a = 0              Zero or positive full C-slope; for   Static-coordinate separation and a local con-
                           θ > 0, causal interior or bound-     strained comparison.
                           ary
 λ<0                       a > 0 or a = 0                       Spatial axis prediction, constrained pure or-
                                                                ders, and longitudinal comparisons.


                                                    52
Here θ is the additional conditional axis rate in (4.4), not a spatial slope. The backward gain
identity and reusable scalar comparisons are collected after the stationary case. All projection
bounds below are applied to actual realizing profiles; pure envelopes provide only the ordered costs
and overlap identities of Lemma 3.9.

5.1   Stationary aspect
Proposition 5.1. There is no calibrated profile satisfying (5.1) with λ = 0.
Proof. Here 0 ≤ a = b ≤ 1, and we use the isotropic profile F (u, z, Y ) of (3.8), on z ≤ u + Y . The
reference-axis label is retained with the exact entropy cancellation (3.7); the later choice of a strip
axis will also retain its earlier parent. Suppose first that a > 0. Take a generic tangent on the
calibrated line and apply Lemma 3.9, with u the only negative coordinate. Its pure profile is P u,
where P is constant and ∂u F ≥ P throughout the domain. For T > 0, run backwards from (0, 0, 0)
to (T, 0, −T ), keeping z = 0, with the frozen velocity cutoff in the upper envelope sufficiently fine for
this segment. The remaining time rate is at least s, so the support inequality and upper envelope
give
                                 (2 − q∗ )T ≤ F (T, 0, −T ) ≤ (P − s)T.
Consequently
                            q∗ ≥ s + 2 − P,       and hence         P > 1 − s.
    On the early side of the calibrated line, namely u + aY < 0, z = u + Y , the support inequality
and equality on the line imply an average bound ∂u F + ∂z F ≤ 2 when integrating toward the line
at fixed Y . Since ∂u F ≥ P > 1 − s, there is a generic aligned point on this side with position rate
E := ∂z F < 1 + s. Lemma 3.7 gives an affine tangent in the three isotropic coordinates there;
denote its time rate by τ ≥ s. Simultaneously retain a tangent at the calibrated point with the
same velocity depth u, whose time depth is −u/a. This is later than the early point and has finer
position depth. The two sites have the same physical velocity units, and the later profile still satisfies
∂u F ≥ P .
    If a = 0, first peel off the constant coordinate on the line u = 0, z = Y . In a tangent,

                                    F (u, z, Y ) = J(u) + H0 (z, Y ),

where the second factor is defined on the full projected (z, Y )-plane. At a further generic point on
the line, the two-coordinate order rule makes H0 affine on each order half-plane. On z ≥ Y , write

                                         H0 (z, Y ) = Ez + τ Y.

Then τ ≥ s and E + τ = q∗ , so E < 1 + s. At Y = 0, u = z > 0, the support inequality gives

                                           J(u) ≥ (2 − E)u.                                         (5.2)

The following projection block is based at the origin in this case. Choose any fixed point on
u = 0, z = Y with Y > 0 as the later site; the tests near it will use velocity depths near zero.
   We give the common block argument. Write m for its base and let b0 ∈ R2 be normalized velocity.
Let (d, c0 ) ∈ R × R2 be normalized time and position in the fine-time frame, after subtracting known
translations. Position in the parent frame is therefore

                                         Ab0 (d, c0 ) := c0 − db0

to mesh accuracy. The profile and its regularization give the following properties inside typical
parents.

                                                   53
  (i) Velocity has a Frostman ball bound of some exponent strictly greater than 1 − s. For a > 0
      this follows from ∂u F ≥ P ; for a = 0 it follows from (5.2) at every tested prefix depth.
 (ii) Velocity cells and time-position cells have dense product incidence. Indeed the refinements
      (u, z, Y ) = (1, 0, 0), (0, 1, 1), and (1, 1, 1) have additive joint exponents. The time-position
      exponent is E + τ .
 (iii) Given a fine velocity bin b0 , the image under Ab0 has a Frostman E bound and at most
       mE+o(1) bins. The fiber in one image bin has at most mτ +o(1) time-position cells. These follow
       by testing u = 1, 0 ≤ z ≤ 1, Y = 0, and the joint refinement, rebasing with the known velocity.
    Suppose that every strip of power-small width has power-small velocity mass. By Lemma 4.3,
applied with a slightly smaller ball exponent at most one, directions b − b0 have an angular Frostman
exponent σ > 1 − s outside removable exceptional pairs. Product density and Cauchy–Schwarz give
a pin b0 and a dense set of such b’s that share a dense set of time-position cells with b0 . We may
also arrange |b − b0 | ≥ m−o(1) .
    Fix one of these overlaps. Keep its rich Ab -bins, each containing at least mτ −o(1) cells. The
support upper bound for Ab shows that these retain full overlap exponent E + τ . Inside a fixed
Ab -bin the different cells give, up to bounded mesh multiplicity, different d-bins: once d and Ab are
given, c0 = Ab + db is known to the tested accuracy. Moreover

                                         Ab0 = Ab + (b − b0 )d.

Since |b − b0 | ≥ m−o(1) , the rich bin therefore yields at least mτ −o(1) distinct Ab0 -bins in one thick
row parallel to b − b0 . Select perpendicular row labels separated by a sufficiently large mesh constant.
Their collections of point bins are disjoint up to bounded multiplicity. There are at most mE+o(1)
point bins in all, so the perpendicular projection of the retained Ab0 ’s has at most mE−τ +o(1) bins.
    This restriction is dense for the Frostman image law, not merely large in cardinality. Indeed full
overlap exponent E + τ , together with the fiber upper bound τ , gives E − o(1) image exponent.
The typical image bins have pre-trim weights at least m−E−o(1) , by Lemma 3.2. Thus the image
restriction has subpower mass. We can consequently apply Lemma 4.2 to these direction-dependent
restrictions. It is contradicted by

                         E − τ < E,       E − τ < 1,       E − τ < (E + σ)/2.

The first inequality uses τ ≥ s > 0, the second uses E < 1 + s, and the third follows from
E − 2τ < 1 − s < σ. If E − τ < 0, the support upper bound itself is already impossible.
    It follows that, in a dense family of parents, a dense amount of velocity mass lies in strips of width
   −χ
m for some fixed χ > 0, after subselection. Here the choice need not use one exceptionally rare
parent. To see this, average the maximal conditional strip mass over the parents, using finite grids
and bounded strip enlargements. If no fixed width power had subpower average along a subsequence,
each such average would be power-small. Markov’s inequality and diagonal selection over countably
many width powers would produce typical parents with all strips power-light, contrary to the block
argument. We retain the selected strip mass across the parents, choosing its axis separately in each
parent.
    Choose a fixed neighborhood of the later site in which the time and position cutoffs remain
finer than those of the earlier parent. Use the resulting parallel and normal coordinates there, and
include the earlier parent label. At later isotropic states with positive velocity refinement, this label
is recoverable up to bounded ambiguity. Both time and position are finer than at the earlier parent;
rebasing position backwards multiplies velocity uncertainty by at most the earlier time resolution.
The label includes the earlier time bin, spatial bins, and any fixed original slope label. Once it is

                                                   54
known, the strip axis is fixed, and switching isotropic grids costs only bounded multiplicity. Thus
the later isotropic profile and calibration are preserved under the dense restriction.
    The conditional time rate is also preserved. Given the base label, the fixed original slope label,
and a later time prefix, the earlier parent time and its spatial bins, hence its selected axis, are
already determined. Conditioning on these extras therefore consumes no further time increment.
The linear frame change in velocity is used for position in the matched units as well: position units
are velocity units times the corresponding time unit. In the two-site construction only the time and
position units change between the sites. This retains the scalar shear relation. Lemma 3.6 allows
these tests to be realized simultaneously, with errors negligible in the final block units.
    Now take mixed velocity depths l1 , l2 ∈ (0, χ), with position depths li + Y . Given the parent, the
normal velocity rate is zero throughout this depth window. The parallel velocity rate is at least P if
a > 0, and at least J ′ (l1 ) almost everywhere if a = 0. For completeness, this lower bound is valid
also at the aligned faces used in projection tests. Compare a short parallel increment with the same
increment after making time and both position cutoffs finer, with the two position cutoffs equal and
with strict slack. At the finer state change the normal velocity cutoff to the parallel cutoff at both
endpoints; this costs zero while both depths remain in (0, χ). The resulting isotropic increment
has the asserted lower rate. Dropping the extra conditioning can only increase that increment by
submodularity. Valid interior comparisons pass to the aligned tangents by continuity.
    Whenever this parallel rate exceeds 1 − s, its aligned position rate equals one by the scalar
projection test (4.3), now in the fixed axes. The distinct velocity, position, and time forms can be
peeled at generic mixed aligned states, so these are full partial rates there.
    For a > 0, start on the later calibrated path with equal velocity depths l1 = l2 = l ∈ (0, χ).
Choose the backward duration small enough to stay in the chosen neighborhood and to keep both
depths in (0, χ). Hold the parallel depth l1 fixed and increase the normal depth l2 at speed one.
Then the normal position depth l2 + Y is fixed, while the parallel position depth decreases at
speed one. The normal velocity has zero rate, so time contributes at least s and parallel position
contributes one. The advancing normal depth is the larger width depth and therefore has coefficient
1 − κ in the ellipse support term. Thus the profile minus the ellipse width term decreases backwards
at rate at least
                                               2 + s − κ.                                          (5.3)
To integrate generic derivatives, perturb the isotropic starting depth and starting time slightly. The
resulting segments sample all three mixed alignment parameters. Integrate on generic segments
and pass back by Lipschitz continuity. Parent recoverability holds throughout because the starting
velocity depths remain inside the strip window. The initial equality and support now contradict
(5.1).
    For a = 0, (5.2) supplies arbitrarily small positive l with J ′ (l) > 1 − s, on a set of positive
measure. Fix a positive comparison duration within the chosen later neighborhood and smaller
than χ/2. For each sufficiently small such generic l, set both initial depths to l and use generic
later starting times. The error of the initial isotropic state against the calibrated value is O(l), and
including the parent adds no error in exponent. Sending l to zero with the duration fixed again
contradicts (5.1), since 2 + s − κ > 1 + 2s − 10κ for s ≤ 1.

5.2    Gain and scalar backward comparisons
Henceforth λ ̸= 0. Set

                  u∗ = 1 − s,      h = (1 + s)/2,        kC = 1 + κ,     kB = 1 − κ.



                                                    55
Testing states are fully aligned unless indicated otherwise:

                            R = B − C,          D = C + Y,          A = B + Y.

Put H = F − kC C − kB B, and write z(y) for a fully aligned state with Y = y. A backward segment
of duration T starts at z(0) and ends at z(−T ); its gain is H(z(0)) − H(z(−T )). Prescribe velocities
by C ′ = −v1 , B ′ = −v2 , where primes denote forward y-derivatives. Recall that

                         (p, n1 , e, g, t, r) = (∂C F, ∂B F, ∂D F, ∂A F, ∂Y F, ∂R F ).

The forward y-derivative of H(z(y)) is

                 t + (v1 − v2 )r − v1 p − v2 n1 + (1 − v1 )e + (1 − v2 )g + kC v1 + kB v2 .         (5.4)

Indeed R′ = v1 − v2 , D′ = 1 − v1 , and A′ = 1 − v2 . Integrating this derivative over [−T, 0] gives the
gain. With the increasing backward clock τ = −y, the potential in Lemma 3.11 is −H(z(−τ )). At
a calibrated start, referenced to zero, (3.5) gives H(z(−T )) ≥ −q∗ T , so the gain is at most q∗ T . If
an upper envelope U ≥ F from (3.9) agrees with F at the start, its endpoint gain is a lower bound
for the actual gain. An initial error tending to zero is harmless.
    We make precise how rates on generic sheets will be used. A singleton rate obtained by peeling is
a constant full partial in that tangent; a group rate is the derivative along its specified diagonal. To
integrate an inequality valid on a sheet-open region, first integrate on almost all slightly translated
parallel segments and then pass to the chosen segment by Lipschitz continuity. This also transfers
an inequality to a boundary approached from the indicated side. Subsequent off-sheet tests of a
singleton-affine tangent retain that singleton rate.
    In particular, let Γ be the fully aligned plane R = λY when λ > 0. For λ ̸= 1, its spatial partials
are singleton constants in a generic first tangent, and the parameter diagonal (Y, R) 7→ (Y +z, R+λz)
has rate at least s. The latter lower bound can be tested with spatial cutoffs fixed in the interior
of the separated tangent. Projection tests on its two sides supply the subscripts s and θ of (4.4)
simultaneously. When λ = 1, the corresponding grouped conclusion is (4.10). These facts hold in
tangents based anywhere on the tie, not only on the equality line.

Lemma 5.2 (Scalar comparison). Suppose a local restriction of an inherited profile has grouped
coordinates X, y, Z, with domain Z ≤ X + y, and no other local constraints. The groups consist of
disjoint nonnegative coordinate increments of the underlying profile; additional fixed coordinates are
allowed. At generic aligned points write their rates as dX , dy , dZ . Suppose, also in the tangent tests
at the path points under consideration, that

                                dy ≥ t0 ,       dX > u∗ /2 =⇒ dZ ≥ h.                               (5.5)

For k ∈ {kC , kB }, the gain of this scalar profile minus kX can reach rate t0 + h − κ, with arbitrarily
small further loss. More precisely, along every path on Z = X + y, parametrized by y and with
−X ′ (y) ∈ [ρ, 1 − ρ], almost every point admits arbitrarily short straight backward comparisons with
this rate, up to a loss tending to zero with ρ, and with velocity in the same interval.

Proof. Apply Lemma 3.9 separately along the path under consideration. The negative coordinate is
X; y, Z strictly increase. Slack is obtained by shifting y more than Z. At almost every path point
the resulting tangent has pure slope p0 , and dX ≥ p0 . If p0 > u∗ /2, velocity zero gives rate at least
t0 + h by (5.5).



                                                      56
    If p0 ≤ u∗ /2, use velocity one. In centered offsets its ray is X = −y, Z = 0, y < 0. If m ∈ [ρ, 1−ρ]
is the original path speed, choose β ∈ (y/m, y). Then

                             −y > −mβ,          y > β,       0 > (1 − m)β.

These inequalities give the overlap condition of Lemma 3.9, locally up to alignment. Thus dX = p0
on the comparison ray, and integration gives rate at least

                            t0 + k − p0 ≥ t0 + 1 − κ − u∗ /2 = t0 + h − κ.

Perturb velocities zero and one into the permitted interval. The loss is arbitrarily small by the
Lipschitz bounds. The strict comparison in the tangent gives arbitrarily short comparisons in the
original profile. Any additional linear contribution to the gain is included in these same endpoint
calculations.

Lemma 5.3 (Longitudinal comparisons). The following two comparisons are available locally, and
in inherited path tangents, under the stated derivative and conditional-time bounds.
  (i) Low longitudinal rate. Fix v1 = 1, so D = d′0 is constant. Suppose the backward cost of the
      varying C-term has been bounded by P ≤ u∗ /2 and removed. This may be done in the pure
      upper envelope when B is not negative, or where ∂C F = P exactly. If t ≥ s in the local region,
      the total gain can reach 1 + 2s − κ, with arbitrarily small further loss.
 (ii) High longitudinal rate. Fix v1 = 0, so C = c′0 is constant. Suppose p ≥ P > u∗ /2 in
      neighborhoods of the aligned states being visited, up to the domain constraints, and t ≥ s. The
      same gain 1 + 2s − κ is available.
Proof. For the low comparison use the scalar groups

                              X = B,        y = Y,        Z = A = R + d′0 .

For the upper envelope (3.9), the remaining function is

                               F + (Q) = F (n(−H0 ), Q) − G(n(−H0 )).

Its rates are those of the actual frozen-cutoff slice Q 7→ F (n(−H0 ), Q), since the subtracted term
is constant. Choose H0 sufficiently large for all the following compact tests; in particular C is
fixed sufficiently fine in this slice. Any additional negative D is held fixed in the pure part; in the
remaining part it is either d′0 or fixed finer, with D < C + Y . In the exact-slope version F − P C is
locally independent of C. Freeze C at a slightly finer value to obtain strict slack for B < C + R and
D < C + Y ; on alignment this auxiliary slice agrees with the remainder up to a constant.
    To verify (5.5), retain R, A separately with A ≤ D + R, A ≤ B + Y , while the other faces have
slack. At a generic scalar aligned point in any path tangent, peel off the singleton rates for B, Y ;
the (R, A)-summand has affine diagonal rate. Increase R separately to open A < D + R, retaining
A = B + Y . The time-projection block for (B, Y, A) gives g ≥ fs (n1 ). Its fixed D-measurement
rebases under time refinement with bounded ambiguity because C is fine. The two marginal laws
and their dense product relation are the comparable-cutoff laws of Lemma 3.2; thus this is the
actual sheared block test. If dX = n1 > u∗ /2, it follows that g ≥ h. The direction increasing B, R, A
together preserves A = B + Y and has rate n1 + r + g ≥ n1 + h. Integrate this inequality on the
displaced matching slices and pass back by continuity to the (R, A)-grouped slice. There the same
direction has rate n1 + dZ . The peeled singleton n1 is constant on both slices, so subtraction gives
dZ ≥ h. The conditional parameter increment with C, D fixed gives dy ≥ s.

                                                     57
   These constructions also apply after the signed-path tangent: take simultaneous tangents of the
indicated auxiliary slices, which have actual realizing states. When freezing C is only local, cover
the open path portion by countably many neighborhoods with a single valid frozen value on each.
Lemma 5.2 with t0 = s now gives total rate at least

                                 s + h − κ + kC − P ≥ 1 + 2s − κ.

   For the high comparison the scalar groups are

                        X = B = R + c′0 ,       y = Y = D − c′0 ,     Z = A.

At full-aligned generic states, e ≥ fs (p) ≥ h. Hence the y-group has rate at least s + h. If g < h,
the projection inequalities imply n1 ≤ u∗ /2. They also force r = 0: otherwise g ≥ fr (e) ≥ h, a
contradiction.
    We justify transfer to the grouped slice, including its path tangents. At a generic scalar aligned
point, first peel off the fixed coordinate C by a direction increasing every other coordinate; then
separate the X, y, Z groups. Their distinct forms are positive on a common vector. The A-rate
is therefore a constant singleton in this first tangent. Test full-aligned generic points on nearby
translated sheets, allowing C to vary. The aligned y-direction also advances A; subtracting its
constant rate transfers dy = t+e ≥ s+h. If this singleton rate is less than h, the aligned X-direction,
with the same subtraction, transfers dX = n1 + r ≤ u∗ /2. Thus (5.5) holds with t0 = s + h, and
Lemma 5.2 gives s + 2h − κ = 1 + 2s − κ.

   Whenever a prescribed-length comparison is needed in a free region, apply Lemma 3.11 to
these pathwise short steps, taking ρ small. If the construction occurs inside a tangent, its endpoint
comparison is enough: the effective straight velocity lies in the convex velocity box, and the
comparison passes back to arbitrarily short original steps. A linear closed half-space constraint
passes back as well. Pure backward cost bounds may be integrated with the remaining gain, or
subtracted at the final endpoint from an upper envelope that is exact at the start. Choose the
additional endpoint, clipping, and control losses so that, together with the loss already incurred in
the selected comparison, the total is less than 10κ.

5.3   Increasing aspect with positive longitudinal speed
Proposition 5.4. There is no calibrated profile satisfying (5.1) with λ > 0 and a > 0.

Proof. Take the conditional parameter tangent (4.4) and a further simultaneous signed-envelope
tangent on the calibrated line. The negative coordinates are C, together with B if b > 0 and D if
a > 1. Their pure domain is full: the parameters R, Y in mixed constraints increase, and negative
cutoffs at an earlier path time can be paired with the other cutoffs at a later time. Large positive
shifts in R, Y open the required slack. On compact aligned tests all negatives can be replaced by
n(−H0 ) in (3.9) for sufficiently large H0 . When relevant, the differences C − B and C − D of the
shifted negatives increase strictly, since a > b and a > a − 1.
    Let P ∈ [0, 1] be the pure marginal rate of C. If P = 0, test the upper envelope backwards for
unit time with v1 = M0 , v2 = 0, where M0 is large and fixed. The C-cost is zero, B is constant, and
Y, A, R decrease backwards. The only possible positive backward cost is that of D, at most M0 − 1,
whether it belongs to the pure or remaining profile. The gain is at least

                                   kC M0 − (M0 − 1) = 1 + κM0 ,


                                                  58
contradicting support for large M0 . Comparisons along binding faces follow by translating the
segments into the relative interior first. Henceforth P > 0.
   We divide the remaining proof into three cases. When θ = 0, we use longitudinal comparisons,
upper-envelope tests, and full-alignment projection bounds. When θ > 0 and λ ̸= 1, we use the
projection inequalities on the tied plane and then separate the possible velocities. When θ > 0 and
λ = 1, the coincident spatial forms require the grouped projection bounds.

No additional conditional axis rate
First suppose θ = 0, so t ≥ s everywhere. If b ≤ 0 and P ≤ u∗ /2, apply the low comparison of
Lemma 5.3 to the upper envelope starting at zero. Its pure cost along v1 = 1 is at most P , and
any additional negative D is constant. If b ≤ 0 and P > u∗ /2, use the high comparison in the
actual profile. On its backward paths from C = 0, the pure C-rate is P : if D is negative, its value
D = y < 0 is strictly coarser than C = 0 in normalized negative units. Thus p ≥ P locally at the
visited states. Both comparisons contradict support.
    Suppose b > 0, and let Q1 be the pure marginal of B. If P + Q1 ≤ u∗ , the upper-envelope test
v1 = v2 = 1 keeps D, A, R fixed and costs at most P + Q1 in the pure part. The remaining time
rate is at least s, so its gain is at least 2 + s − (P + Q1 ) ≥ 1 + 2s. If Q1 = 0 and P > u∗ , the pure
profile is independent of B, and the high comparison has p ≥ P locally at its visited states, with
any negative D strictly coarser.
    The remaining possibility is P, Q1 > 0 and P + Q1 > u∗ . Consider the aligned sheet

                              C = −aα,         B = −bα,           R = B − C,
                                                                                                  (5.6)
                               Y = y,       D = C + y,           A = B + y.

Initially restrict to y < α. The free y-direction increases precisely D, A, Y , so at a generic point
peeling splits off (C, B, R). The remaining three forms are distinct, since a, b > 0 and a ̸= b.
Therefore e, g, t are singleton constants in a first tangent. Keeping Y fixed, use the two perturbations

                   (C, B, D, A, Y, R) 7−→ (C + δ, B, D + δ, A, Y, R − δ),
                                                                                 δ > 0.           (5.7)
                   (C, B, D, A, Y, R) 7−→ (C, B + δ, D, A + δ, Y, R + δ),

They preserve full alignment and make C, respectively B, strictly finest among these two normalized
negative depths. Any negative D is strictly coarser because y < α, and remains so for sufficiently
small δ. The pure lower bounds give p ≥ P on the first order side and n1 ≥ Q1 on the second. The
full-aligned projection tests therefore give the following bounds for the same singleton constants
e, g, t:
                                e ≥ fs (P ),   g ≥ fs (Q1 ),   t ≥ s.
The two clipped position bounds sum to at least 1+s: if neither clips their sum is 2s+P +Q1 > 1+s;
if one clips the other is at least s. At fixed α, the gain is at least 1 + 2s. Integrate along generic
sheet segments and let α → 0. This gives the contradiction on the backward ray v1 = v2 = 0, y < 0.

The tied plane for θ > 0, λ ̸= 1
Now assume θ > 0, λ ̸= 1. At generic points of Γ, the parameter diagonal rate is at least s, and

                      e ≥ fs (p),     g ≥ fs (n1 ),        n1 ≥ fθ (p),   g ≥ fθ (e).             (5.8)

Set
                        KL = p + e,        KN = n1 + g,           KΣ = KL + KN .

                                                      59
In particular KN ≥ KL and g ≥ e. Parameterize Γ by L0 = C + ay and y, and average over thin
strips on each side of L0 = 0 in a fixed time interval. Integrating the support bound from the
equality line gives average KΣ ≤ 2 on the minus side and average KΣ ≥ 2 on the plus side. For the
gain on the tie with v1 = m, v2 = m − λ, formula (5.4) becomes

             DΓ (m) = parameter diagonal rate + e + g + m(2 − KΣ ) + λ(KN − 1 + κ).                (5.9)

The strip average of DΓ (a) tends to q∗ on either side as the strip shrinks, by calibration and Lipschitz
continuity. The plus-side average of KN is at least one.
    Write P, N− , E− for the minus-side pure rates of the negative coordinates C, B, D, using zero
for an absent negative coordinate. Here the C-rate is its marginal P , by the ordering of L0 divided
by the negative speeds. These are lower bounds for the actual partials. On the plus side let the
pure rates be p+ , N+ , E+ . They are exact actual negative partials there. Indeed all negative cutoffs
are strictly finer than the path at time y, while the other cutoffs are finer or equal; replacing the
latter by their path values is valid. Increase all negatives strictly, with the C increment at least as
large as those of any negative B, D. This preserves the constraints at both tuples. Overlap gives
equality of the increment with its pure value. Each partial already has its pure affine order slope as
a lower bound; in the peeled tangent, equality of this positive combination forces individual equality.
Interior derivative comparisons pass to these tangents also at alignment.
    The weighted pure rate does not depend on the order:

                   J = aP + b+ N− + (a − 1)+ E− = ap+ + b+ N+ + (a − 1)+ E+ .                     (5.10)

The negative speeds in this use are distinct because λ ̸= 1. Marginal maximality gives p+ ≤ P . The
minus-side averages imply

                            P + max(P + θ, N− ) ≤ u∗ ,        P + E− ≤ 1.                         (5.11)

Here is the clipping argument. Since KN ≥ KL , the average of KL on the minus side is at most one.
If P + s ≥ 1, then KL ≥ P + 1 > 1, a contradiction. Put l = max(fθ (P ), N− ) > 0. Then

                                    KΣ ≥ P + (P + s) + l + fs (l).

If l + s ≥ 1, this is at least 2 + 2P > 2. Thus l + s < 1, which also gives P + θ < 1 and
l = max(P + θ, N− ). The average bound now gives P + l ≤ 1 − s. Finally KL ≥ P + E− proves the
second inequality. We will use

                            2P ≤ u∗ ,     P + N− ≤ u∗ ,        θ ≤ u∗ − 2P.

When s = 1, these already contradict P > 0. Otherwise the following cases cover the present
parameters.

Case a > 1, b ≤ 0. On the plus side, direct expansion gives

                              DΓ (a) ≥ s + a − J + g + b(1 − KN ) + κλ.

The term with b is nonnegative after averaging, because the plus-side average of KN is at least one.
Also
                                J = aP + (a − 1)E− ≤ a − 1 + P.



                                                   60
If E+ ≥ s + P , then g ≥ e = E+ , giving average gain at least 1 + 2s + κλ. If E+ < s + P , then
p+ + E+ ≤ 2P + s ≤ 1, and therefore

                             J = (a − 1)(p+ + E+ ) + p+ ≤ a − 1 + p+ .

When p+ > 0, g ≥ e ≥ fs (p+ ) = s + p+ , again giving the required gain. When p+ = 0,
choose fixed M0 > a large and use v1 = M0 , v2 = 0 in the upper envelope. The negative tuple
(C, D) = (M0 , M0 − 1) has the plus order, since
                                            M0 − 1   M0
                                                   >    .
                                            a−1      a
Its pure cost is at most M0 − 1, and the varying remaining coordinates decrease backwards. The
gain is at least 1 + κM0 , contradicting support for large M0 .

Case a ≥ 1, b > 0. Here 0 < b ≤ 1. The exact plus-side rates give

                         DΓ (a) ≥ s + a + b − J + (1 − b)g + κλ
                                ≥ s + 1 − P + b(1 − N− ) + (1 − b)g + κλ,

using J ≤ a − 1 + P + bN− . We have 1 − N− ≥ s + P . If N+ ≥ P , then g ≥ fs (N+ ) ≥ s + P
pointwise. If N+ < P , use p+ ≤ P , e ≤ g, and exactness to get KΣ ≤ 2P + 2g. Since its plus-side
average is at least two, the average of g is at least 1 − P ≥ s + P . In both alternatives the average
gain is at least 1 + 2s + κλ. At a = 1, D is absent from the negative group and its coefficient is
zero, so the same displayed calculation applies.

Case 0 < a ≤ 1, b ≤ 0, λ > 1. Only C is negative, and p = P exactly on the plus side.
Formula (5.9) gives
                         DΓ (a) − DΓ (1) = (a − 1)(2 − KΣ ),
whose plus-side average is nonnegative. Moreover,

                          DΓ (1) ≥ s + 1 − P + g + (λ − 1)(KN − 1) + κλ.

Here g ≥ e ≥ s + P , and the last KN − 1 term is nonnegative in average. This again gives average
gain at least 1 + 2s + κλ.

Case 0 < b < a < 1. We have 0 < λ < 1, and the negatives are C, B. The plus-side rate N+ = Q1
is the B-marginal. It is positive: if Q1 = 0, then N− = 0, and (5.10) gives p+ = P > 0, contradicting
n1 = 0 and n1 ≥ fθ (p) on the plus side.
    If P + Q1 ≥ u∗ , use (5.6) with α < y. This is strictly causal, since R = λα < λY . At a generic
point its first tangent has singleton constants e, g, t. Apply the two perturbations (5.7), taking
δ < λ(y − α) to preserve the strict causal inequality. The two negative orders give

                               e ≥ s + P,      g ≥ fs (Q1 ),    t ≥ s;

the first bound is unclipped because 2P ≤ u∗ . For increasing α, y together, the gain rate is at least

                       a + b + κλ + s − J + (1 − a)(s + P ) + (1 − b)fs (Q1 ).                  (5.12)

To justify the cost J at the negative-order tie, perturb within the tangent to B/b > C/a, equivalently
C + aR/λ > 0 on alignment. At β = R/λ both negatives are strictly finer than the calibrated path,

                                                 61
and all positives are finer or equal; in particular Y > β, with the corresponding position margins.
Replacing positives by their path values is valid, still with B = C + R. Ordered increases of C, B,
with the C-increment at least as large, preserve validity. Overlap gives exact negative rates on this
open order side, with weighted sum J; these comparisons pass to the tangent. The remaining axis
term λr is nonnegative. Integrate the derivative bound on perturbed parallel segments that retain
strict order, then approach the tie. This proves (5.12). Parallel sheets approaching α = y transfer it
to the calibrated rate.
    If Q1 ≥ u∗ , subtract 1 + 2s from (5.12), using fs (Q1 ) = 1, to obtain

                      κλ + a(u∗ − P ) − bN− + (1 − a)P
                           = κλ + a(u∗ − P − N− ) + (a − b)N− + (1 − a)P ≥ 0.

If 0 < Q1 < u∗ , the difference is

             κλ + P + Q1 − u∗ + a(u∗ − 2P ) + b(u∗ − N− − Q1 )
                = κλ + (1 − b)(P + Q1 − u∗ ) + (a − b)(u∗ − 2P ) + b(u∗ − P − N− ) ≥ 0.

Each term has the stated sign by (5.11).
    If instead P + Q1 ≤ u∗ , put A0 = u∗ − (p+ + Q1 ) ≥ 0. The upper-envelope test v1 = v2 = 1 has
pure cost p+ + Q1 , because its negative tuple C = B = 1 has the plus order. The remaining time
rate is at least s − λθ. Its gain minus 1 + 2s is therefore at least

                                                 A0 − λθ.

For a second test follow DΓ (1) backwards from zero. Its ray has L0 = (a − 1)y > 0 for y < 0, so
p = p+ , n1 = Q1 exactly, and g ≥ s + Q1 , since 0 < Q1 < u∗ . Formula (5.9) gives gain minus 1 + 2s
at least
                                     A0 − λ(u∗ − 2Q1 ) + κλ.
One test suffices. If Q1 ≥ u∗ /2, the second expression is nonnegative. Otherwise set d = u∗ −
2 max(P, Q1 ) ≥ 0. Then

                        A0 ≥ u∗ − P − Q1 ≥ d,          0 ≤ min(θ, u∗ − 2Q1 ) ≤ d.

Since λ < 1, the larger of the two lower bounds is nonnegative even after discarding κλ.

Case 0 < a < 1, b ≤ 0, 0 < λ < 1. Only C is negative, with 0 < P ≤ u∗ /2. Start at
C = B = L > 0, Y = 0 on alignment, where L is arbitrarily small and generic, and run backwards
for unit time with

             C = L − y,       B = L + x,        v2 = −x′ ∈ [ρ, 1 − ρ],       R = x + y ≤ λy.          (5.13)

Choose ρ small also relative to 1 − λ. These paths satisfy 0 ≤ x ≤ −y. At y < 0, the actual C-partial
is P locally by overlap. Indeed R ≥ y and λ ≥ a give R/λ ≥ y/a. Choose β ∈ ((y − L)/a, y/a).
Then C > −aβ, and each of

                    B = L + x,       D = L,   A = L + x + y,      Y = y,    R=x+y

is strictly finer than its path value at β. For B this uses b ≤ 0; for D, A, Y it uses a < 1, b ≤ 0, β < y/a;
for R it uses the preceding inequality. The comparison is valid in a neighborhood up to alignment.


                                                    62
    In the strict causal interior t ≥ s, so the exact-slope low comparison gives the pathwise short
steps. On the boundary R = λy, almost every y is a peeling point of Γ for generic L: the map
(L, y) 7→ (C, Y ) = (L − y, y) is invertible, so Fubini applies. This exceptional set in y is independent
of the path, since its boundary state is determined by L, y. In the first tangent there, p = P and
g ≥ s + P . Use v1 = 1, v2 = 0 to enter the strict causal side backwards. There t ≥ s, r ≥ 0, and the
spatial rates are constant throughout the tangent. The gain reaches

                                     s + kC − P + g ≥ 1 + 2s + κ.

Perturb v2 to ρ < 1 − λ to retain the inward direction and the strict gain. Lemma 3.11 gives the
endpoint contradiction, since the starting error tends to zero with L.

The coincident tie λ = 1
Finally suppose λ = 1, θ > 0, with P > 0. Use paths on Γ through zero with v1 = m ∈ [ρ, 1 − ρ],
v2 = m − 1. At y < 0 they have C > 0, D = B < 0. Thus p ≥ P locally: C is strictly finest if D, B
are also negative. Use the grouped domain

                      B = D = Z1 ≤ C + Y,          A = Z2 ≤ Z1 + Y,        R = Y.

Its parameter diagonal rate is at least s. The Z1 -diagonal rate H and the Z2 -rate g satisfy (4.10) at
generic aligned states and in the relevant inherited tangents. The slice retains the binding alignment
required by the tied projection argument.
    Along each path, C alone decreases, while Y, Z1 , Z2 strictly increase. Take its signed tangent,
with positive shifts of these latter coordinates opening both constraints, and let p0 be the pure
C-slope. Exactness in overlap somewhere, together with the inherited p ≥ P , gives p0 ≥ P > 0.
If p0 ≤ u∗ , test v1 = 1, v2 = 0 on the tie. Its ray is C = −y, Z1 = 0, Z2 = y; a path time strictly
between y/m and y gives exact p = p0 , since the other grouped cutoffs are strictly finer. Its gain is
at least
                       s + kC − p0 + g ≥ s + kC − p0 + (s + p0 ) = 1 + 2s + κ.
If p0 > u∗ , use v1 = 0, v2 = −1. The pure lower bound and (4.10) give H, g ≥ 1, so the gain is at
least
                              s + H + 2g − kB ≥ 2 + s + κ ≥ 1 + 2s + κ.
Integrate along nearby generic tie segments and perturb the endpoint values m = 0, 1 into [ρ, 1 − ρ],
retaining v2 = m − 1. The tie and velocity box are preserved. These are the required pathwise
control steps, giving the final contradiction in this case.

Lemma 5.5 (Local constrained comparison). Let an inherited full profile be based on the parameter
tie, with 0 < λ < 1, θ > 0, the conditional profile (4.4), and the inherited tie projection inequalities.
Suppose its full C-partial is a constant P ∈ (0, u∗ /2]. Without requiring calibration of this profile,
there is a backward endpoint comparison from its origin, respecting R ≤ λY , whose gain per unit
time is at least 1 + 2s − 4κ. Its velocity is in a box with v1 = 1 and v2 ∈ [ρ, 1 − ρ], for sufficiently
small ρ > 0.

Proof. Use the paths (5.13) from a small generic C = B = L, Y = 0. In the strict causal interior
the constant full C-slope and t ≥ s supply the low longitudinal comparison. At generic boundary
points, the tie inequalities give g ≥ s + P , and the spatial singleton rates in the first tangent are
constant. The backward direction v1 = 1, v2 = 0 enters the strict causal side and has gain at least
s + kC − P + g ≥ 1 + 2s + κ. Perturbing to v2 = ρ < 1 − λ retains this strict gain. The generic-L

                                                   63
argument in Proposition 5.4 gives these steps almost everywhere along every admissible path,
including its boundary epochs. Lemma 3.11 therefore gives a fixed-duration endpoint comparison,
with arbitrarily small additional loss.
    Translate both endpoints by the same aligned depth shift to move the start from L to zero.
This changes the gain by O(L); it does not alter R, Y , so it preserves the half-space. Choose L
and the further clipping and control losses so that, together with the κ-loss in the low longitudinal
comparison, the total loss per unit time is less than 4κ. The straight endpoint displacement lies
in the convex velocity box and respects the half-space. It consequently certifies a short step upon
passing back from a tangent.

5.4   Increasing aspect with zero longitudinal speed
Proposition 5.6. There is no calibrated profile satisfying (5.1) with λ > 0 and a = 0.

Proof. Here b = −λ. Take (4.4) and peel off the constant coordinate C in a generic calibrated
tangent; all other coordinates strictly increase. Its additive contribution is a function of C alone.
Use aligned backward paths through zero with

                               ρ ≤ v 1 ≤ M0 ,     −(λ + 1) ≤ v2 ≤ 1,

where M0 is fixed sufficiently large relative to λ and 1/κ. If θ > 0, impose R ≤ λY . Along every
such path, the C-summand is differentiable almost everywhere because C moves monotonically with
speed at least ρ. In a tangent there its full slope p is constant. We verify the control property in
this tangent.
    If p = 0, take v1 = M0 , v2 = 0. After removing C, the only backward cost is at most M0 − 1,
from D, and the gain is at least 1 + κM0 . Taking M0 ≥ λ also preserves the causal half-space at its
boundary. Suppose p > 0. If θ = 0, or at a strictly causal state, t ≥ s throughout a neighborhood
and its tangents. Use the low longitudinal comparison for p ≤ u∗ /2 and the high comparison for
p > u∗ /2, perturbing v1 = 0 to ρ in the latter.
    It remains to consider boundary states with θ > 0. The path point need not be generic on Γ. Its
tangent nevertheless has constant full C-slope p. Each of the first three cases below chooses a fixed
tie direction using only p, λ. Its gain bound holds at generic points of the tangent’s tie; integrate
on nearby generic parallel tie segments and pass by continuity to the segment from the tangent
origin. This gives the endpoint comparison required by Lemma 3.11. The fourth case supplies such
a comparison directly.
  (i) For λ = 1, use (4.10). If 0 < p ≤ u∗ , the tie test v1 = 1, v2 = 0 has gain at least
      s + kC − p + (s + p) = 1 + 2s + κ. If p > u∗ , the test v1 = 0, v2 = −1 has gain at least
      s + 1 + 2 − kB . Perturb along the tie; in the second test use v1 = ρ, v2 = ρ − 1.
 (ii) For λ ̸= 1, p > u∗ /2, the spatial inequalities (5.8) give e, g ≥ h and KN ≥ KL ≥ 1.
      Formula (5.9) with m = 0 gives at least s + 2h = 1 + 2s. A small tie perturbation makes
      v1 ≥ ρ.
(iii) For λ > 1, 0 < p ≤ u∗ /2, take m = (λ + 1)/2 on the tie. Formula (5.9) gives

                                                       λ−1
                            DΓ (m) ≥ s + 1 − p + g +       (KN − KL ) + κλ.
                                                        2
      Here g ≥ p + s, since p + s ≤ h ≤ 1, and the difference term is nonnegative. The rate is at
      least 1 + 2s, and the velocities fit the chosen box.


                                                 64
 (iv) For 0 < λ < 1, 0 < p ≤ u∗ /2, use Lemma 5.5, with its constant full C-slope P = p. This
      enters or follows the causal region and gives a strict endpoint comparison in the tangent.
All these comparisons, including any secondary control construction inside a tangent, retain a fixed
positive margin over q∗ after the chosen losses. Their endpoint displacements lie in the convex
velocity box and respect the constant half-space constraint. Lemma 3.11 therefore contradicts (3.5)
for the original paths.

5.5   Decreasing aspect
Proposition 5.7. There is no calibrated profile satisfying (5.1) with λ < 0.

Proof. Write c = b − a = −λ > 0. Then 0 ≤ a < b ≤ 1. We have t ≥ s everywhere. The negative
coordinates on the calibrated line are B, R, and C when a > 0. Take the signed-envelope tangent
with the slack shifts described in Lemma 3.9. In this case it is essential to retain the additional
prediction (3.6).
   First suppose a > 0. The pure domain is B ≤ C + R. Let P be the constant pure C-partial
on C/a > max{B/b, R/c}, and let S be the constant pure R-partial on R/c > max{C/a, B/b}.
Lemma 3.9 supplies these constants and makes each a global upper bound for its corresponding
pure partial. On the aligned sector in which C is ahead, C/a > R/c and B = C + R, the pure
profile has the form
                                     G(C, B, R) = P C + Q2 B                                 (5.14)
after referencing at zero, with constant Q2 ≥ 0. To prove this identity, note first that C/a > B/b as
well, since B/b = (a/b)(C/a) + (c/b)(R/c). The pure C-rate is consequently P . The pure R-rate is
zero there. Indeed in the external slices used to construct the pure profile, (3.6) gives flatness in R
when C/a, B/b > R/c; the other coordinates are fixed strictly finer. This flatness holds on an open
order region of the slice tangents and passes to G.
    Thus varying C from the order tie at fixed B, with R = B − C, has slope P . On the tie,
C = (a/b)B, the pure total is affine. Integrating from it proves (5.14). Nonnegativity of the
B-increment gives Q2 ≥ 0. The same B-rate holds locally in the interior: the C, R partials there
are constant, and one can approach the aligned boundary keeping B fixed. Thus the partials of
G near this aligned sector are (P, Q2 , 0) in the (C, B, R) coordinates. The signed-envelope lower
comparison gives p ≥ P and n1 ≥ Q2 for the actual profile.
    The shift to n(−H0 ) in (3.9) is valid on compact aligned tests because a, b, c > 0. If P = 0,
use v1 = M0 , v2 = 0 backwards from zero. The target pure tuple is C = M0 , B = 0, R = −M0 , in
the C-ahead sector, so (5.14) gives zero pure cost. The remaining cost is at most M0 − 1, from D;
the other varying remaining coordinates decrease backwards. Thus the gain is at least 1 + κM0 ,
contradicting support for large fixed M0 .
    If P + Q2 ≤ u∗ , use v1 = v2 = 1. Its pure tuple C = B = 1, R = 0 is again in the C-ahead sector
and costs P + Q2 . The coordinates D, A are fixed, while the remaining time rate is at least s. The
gain is therefore at least 2 + s − (P + Q2 ) ≥ 1 + 2s.
    If P, Q2 > 0 and P + Q2 > u∗ , use the sheet (5.6) with y < α, and the comparison v1 = v2 = 0.
At generic sheet points the same peeling separates e, g, t: the free y-direction first splits off C, B, R,
after which the three remaining forms are distinct. The first perturbation in (5.7) enters the C-ahead
sector while preserving full alignment. Formula (5.14), the pure lower bounds, and the full-aligned
projections give simultaneously

                                e ≥ fs (P ),     g ≥ fs (Q2 ),    t ≥ s.



                                                   65
As above, the two positive input rates with sum greater than 1 − s force e + g ≥ 1 + s. Integrate on
generic parallel segments and pass to α = 0, obtaining gain at least 1 + 2s.
    The only remaining possibility with a > 0 is P > u∗ , Q2 = 0. On the same sheet the C-ahead
perturbation gives e ≥ 1. If S > 0, the second perturbation in (5.7) enters the R-ahead sector
R/c > C/a, still with B = C + R. There the actual tilt rate is at least S. Since e is a constant
singleton in the first tangent, the tilt projection inequality g ≥ fr (e) gives g ≥ 1. Transfer this to
the sheet and use v1 = v2 = 0, whose gain is at least s + 2 ≥ 1 + 2s.
    If S = 0, the global upper bound for the pure R-partial makes G independent of R. It therefore
projects to an ordinary submodular profile on full (C, B)-space: any (C, B) is feasible after taking
R sufficiently fine, and the value does not depend on that choice. If necessary take a further
simultaneous generic tangent on the calibrated line so that this two-variable pure profile has ordinary
order slopes, by the full-domain order rule of Lemma 3.9. Its lower comparisons with the actual
profile and (5.14) are preserved. If the resulting B marginal is positive, the second perturbation
in (5.7) enters its B-ahead order, which is the R-ahead order on alignment. The normal time
projection gives g ≥ s, while the singleton e ≥ 1 persists. The same zero-velocity test then has gain
at least 1 + 2s. If the B-marginal vanishes, G depends on C alone and has global slope P , by (5.14).
Thus the actual profile satisfies p ≥ P > u∗ /2, and the high longitudinal comparison contradicts
calibration.
    It remains to treat a = 0, so c = b > 0. Put the static coordinate C in the nonnegative group.
The external slices now have strict mixed slack also for B ≤ C + R, and the pure domain in B, R is
full. Prediction (3.6) gives zero pure R-rate on B > R. The two-variable pure order rule therefore
gives
                                    G(B, R) = Q2 B       (B ≥ R),                                 (5.15)
with Q2 ≥ 0; the equality at B = R follows by continuity. In particular this applies on alignment
when C ≥ 0. Both negative coordinates can be shifted to equal finer tied cutoffs in the upper
envelope; the other mixed constraints have large slack after that shift.
    Suppose Q2 ≤ u∗ /2. Test the upper envelope backwards from zero with v2 = 1 and v1 ∈ [ρ, 1 − ρ].
At interior negative times C > 0, while A is fixed. The pure backward cost per unit time is Q2 , by
(5.15). With B, R frozen at their finer tied shift, the remaining scalar coordinates are

                                   X = C,       y = Y,      Z = D,

with A fixed. Because C > 0, the first shear constraint has slack; the only binding local constraint
is D ≤ C + Y . The direct longitudinal velocity-time projection gives e ≥ fs (p), and the time rate is
at least s. The fixed normal measurements rebase with bounded ambiguity by (3.2), with B, R fine.
Thus (5.5) holds with t0 = s, also in the path tangents. The scalar comparison gives s + h − κ; the
additional normal contribution is

                                  kB − Q2 ≥ 1 − κ − u∗ /2 = h − κ.

The total is at least 1 + 2s − 2κ, before an arbitrarily small clipping and control loss, contradicting
(5.1).
     Finally suppose Q2 > u∗ /2. Use actual paths backwards from zero with v2 = 0 and v1 = m ∈
[ρ, 1 − ρ]. At negative times C > 0, so the pure bound gives n1 ≥ Q2 locally and therefore g ≥ h at
generic aligned states and in their inherited tangents. Along each path, C alone decreases; B is
static, while R, Y, D, A strictly increase. Apply the signed rule at almost every point of that path,
with pure C-slope p0 . The required overlap and slack hold: increase R, Y substantially more than
the shifts of the other nonnegative coordinates.


                                                  66
   If p0 > u∗ /2, use v1 = v2 = 0 in this tangent. The pure and projection bounds give e, g ≥ h,
and t ≥ s, yielding 1 + 2s. If p0 ≤ u∗ /2, use v1 = 1, v2 = 0. We verify exactness of p = p0 where the
gain is transferred. The centered comparison ray has

                            C = −y,       B = 0,        R = y,        y < 0.

Perturb to B > 0, R = B − C, with C near −y. Away from y = 0, choose

                               β ∈ (−C/m, (B − C)/m),            β < y.

This is possible for sufficiently small positive B, since at B = 0, C = −y both endpoints tend to
y/m < y. Then C > −mβ, and the remaining cutoffs are strictly finer than their path values

                                   0,   mβ,   β,    (1 − m)β,     β

for B, R, Y, D, A, respectively. In particular R = B − C > mβ; D = C + y is near zero and exceeds
(1 − m)β < 0; and A = B + y > β. Overlap therefore gives exact p = p0 at generic nearby aligned
states. Integrate on these perturbed segments and pass to the ray. Discarding the nonnegative
r-term gives gain at least
                                     s + kC − p0 + h ≥ 1 + 2s + κ.
Clip endpoint velocities into the prescribed box and use Lemma 3.11. This proves the contradiction
in the last decreasing-aspect case.

5.6   Conclusion of the geometric estimate
Completion of the proof of Theorem 2.2. If the claimed exponent bound failed, the calibrated con-
struction would give the profile used at the start of this section. Proposition 5.1 excludes λ = 0.
Propositions 5.4 and 5.6 exclude λ > 0, and Proposition 5.7 excludes λ < 0. These exhaust
a ≥ 0, b ≤ 1, λ = a − b.
    Each comparison is in a fixed tangent problem. Fix the finite parameters and any large auxiliary
velocity bound first. For an upper-envelope comparison, then choose H0 sufficiently large for the
resulting compact test region. The admissible depth perturbations and velocity clipping parameters
may subsequently depend on these choices. The scalar losses are at most 2κ in the uses above, and
the local constrained comparison reserves less than 4κ. Choose the additional endpoint, clipping,
and control losses so that, together with the scalar or local constrained loss already incurred, the
total in the selected comparison is strictly below 10κ. These are alternative comparisons, so their
losses are not summed over the mutually exclusive cases. Static coordinates at a = 1 or b = 1
belong to the nonnegative group, as permitted in Lemma 3.9; no division by a zero negative speed is
used. When s = 1, the branches requiring 0 < P ≤ u∗ /2 are empty, and the zero-rate and high-rate
comparisons cover the remaining possibilities. The finite realizing grids and large-scale limit are
chosen after these finite tests, as in Lemma 3.6. The strict inequality (5.1) is therefore impossible,
proving (2.4).


6     Propagation of oscillatory packets
The principal result of this section is the uniform estimate in Theorem 6.4 for arbitrary finite
complex packet arrays and coordinate masks. We define its exact operators and atomic norms, then
prove the composition, localization, and fourth-power estimates used in Section 7. That section
derives the conditional time profile (2.1) required by geometric propagation from near-extremal
packet arrays themselves.

                                                   67
6.1   The phase and an exact packet frame
The Fourier-series construction and changes of packet frame follow the established wave-packet
framework; see [39, arXiv:math/0210084v2, Section 4 and Lemma 4.1] and [10, arXiv:2104.11188v1,
Section 4.1]. We prove the exact frame identities and coefficient estimates used here.
   A finite partition of the sphere into sufficiently small graph patches reduces the local analysis to
the phase
                                      x · ξ + tϕ(ξ),     ξ ∈ R2 ,
with a smooth elliptic function ϕ; the surface measure factor is absorbed into the data. We fix such
a patch and phase throughout this section. We may extend ϕ to all of R2 so that its Hessian is
uniformly definite and bounded, and all its derivatives of order at least two are bounded. Indeed,
on a patch of radius ρ, replace ϕ outside the patch by its quadratic Taylor polynomial, using a
cutoff supported on the patch of radius 2ρ. The resulting change in the Hessian is O(ρ), since the
Taylor remainder and its first two derivatives are O(ρ3 ), O(ρ2 ), and O(ρ), respectively. Taking ρ
small preserves definiteness. A reflection of the time coordinate exchanges the positive and negative
definite cases. Write
                                             Uξ = −∇ϕ(ξ).
On each fixed bounded frequency region, ξ 7→ Uξ is quantitatively bi-Lipschitz. All constants below
may depend on the fixed phase, the frequency region, and the chosen windows.
    Once this continuation is fixed, the arrays below may use frequency labels anywhere in a fixed
bounded region, including the transition region of the cutoff. Their windows need not lie in the
original spherical patch. The phase and all its derivative bounds remain fixed before the packet
scales vary. The global sphere argument will return to data supported on the original graph patches
in Section 8.
    Let N be dyadic, put δ = N −1 , and use the oscillatory factor

Sm,I is (Lf θ)−2 times the adjoint of Pm,I . The operator norm statements follow.

    The initial scale is m = 1, with the single time interval [0, 1). A single-step operator from factor
1 to factor m consists of synthesis at factor 1, followed by analysis in every terminal time bin,
followed by any coordinate mask. More generally, successive transitions use synthesis independently
in each parent time bin and analysis in its child bins. Each finite array has a specified retained
coordinate set, and an index is called active when it belongs to that set, even if its coefficient is zero.
A coordinate mask projects onto a subset of the retained set. If MI denotes the terminal coordinate
mask, the single-step map is exactly
                                          (Tm d)I = MI Pm,I S1,[0,1) d.
The initial array is arbitrary and need not belong to the range of an analysis operator. Every map
retains the full transition matrix between its active index sets. The localization graphs introduced
later count possible interactions; they do not delete individual matrix entries or summands.
