6.3   Uniform exponents and composition
Definition 6.3 (Admissible families). An admissible family has m → ∞, with m, N dyadic and
m ≤ N ≤ mB for a fixed finite B. Its arrays have finite supports, all their position labels have size
at most mB , and all frequency labels lie in a fixed bounded set. Enlarging B is allowed; it must
remain fixed along each family. Constants controlling the phase and windows are fixed as well. The
same convention applies to diagrams with a fixed finite number of scales.

    The full position lattices are infinite, but their retained portions have polynomial size. At a
factor q ≤ m, the frequency and position bounds permit at most

                                   C(N/q)2 (mB qN )2 = Cm2B N 4

indices per bin, and there are q 2 ≤ m2 bins. The norms are also comparable to the largest coordinate
up to polynomial factors. For example, if one bin has D ≥ 1 retained coordinates and M = maxv |cv |,
the singleton test and the decomposition into coordinate atoms give

                                    w2/3 M ≤ A(c) ≤ Dw2/3 M.

Likewise, M 2 ≤ K(c) ≤ DM 2 , since W (E) ≥ θ2 . These comparisons and the weighted bin norm
show that a coordinate error bounded by an arbitrarily large negative power of m times the largest
input coordinate is negligible in all the norms used here.



                                                    70
   Let γ be the supremum, over admissible families and subsequences, of the limiting upper
exponents in m of
                                 A(Tm d)3
                                           ,    d ̸= 0,                               (6.7)
                                  G(d)3
where Tm is a single-step operator, including any terminal mask. By Lemma 6.2, upper bounds
have the same meaning with A(d)3 in the denominator. An exponent bound is uniform on every
fixed complexity range, with any additional positive power loss: otherwise a sequence violating that
bound would itself be an admissible family. We use mo(1) in precisely this sense. In particular, it
does not assert uniformity when a complexity parameter escapes to infinity along a single family.
    The packet estimate to be proved in Section 7 is the following.
Theorem 6.4 (Uniform propagation of finite packet arrays). Fix 0 < κ < 1/10, the continued phase,
a bounded frequency region, and the window system above. For every fixed admissible complexity
range and every ε > 0, there is a finite constant C such that

                                       A(Tm d)3 ≤ Cm10κ+ε G(d)3                                  (6.8)

for every finite complex initial array d and every terminal coordinate mask in that range. The
same estimate holds with A(d)3 on the right. The constant is uniform in m, N, d, and the masks; it
may depend on κ, ε, the complexity range, the fixed phase and frequency region, and the windows.
Equivalently,
                                             γ ≤ 10κ.                                       (6.9)
    We prove the theorem in Section 7. The next lemmas give the exact composition and analytic
estimates used there.
Lemma 6.5 (Composition). Fix rational numbers 0 = a0 < · · · < aJ = 1 and dyadic factors m0 = 1,

    β +ε

   An estimate with normalized cubed norm constant Cm′′ /m′ becomes

                                       r′′ X

                                       r′ I ′′ ⊂I ′

Multiply by r′ and sum over parent bins. This gives the desired global cubed bound at that transition.
Iterate it, using mask contraction at every stage. Taking logarithms in base m gives the stated
weighted sum of exponents. In particular, a fixed decrease in βj decreases the full exponent by that
amount times the positive logarithmic length aj+1 − aj . At each such fixed split, all normalized
position and frequency ranges still have fixed polynomial complexity in that step’s scale ratio. The
uniformity in Definition 6.3 therefore applies simultaneously to all bins. Finally, the last assertion
follows from the support-preserving atomic decomposition in Lemma 6.2.

Lemma 6.6 (Interaction and finite truncation). Let v be a parent index and b a child index, with
Ib ⊂ Iv . The transition matrix vanishes unless |νb − νv | ≤ Cθ+ , and for every integer L ≥ 0 it obeys

Consequently, for every fixed η > 0, interactions outside the enlarged parent beam of radius mη w−
give an error smaller than any prescribed negative power of m, times the largest input coordinate, on
an admissible family. One may also insert intermediate frame identities and truncate all intermediate
position grids to sufficiently large polynomial ranges, with the same type of error.

Proof. The matrix entry is
                                   Z

Disjoint frequency supports give the angular assertion. On overlap, rescale ξ = νv + θ− ζ. The
amplitude and all its derivatives are uniformly bounded, since θ− ≤ θ+ . After the constant and

The remaining linear frequency is (zb − zv − (tIb − tIv )Uνv )/w− . Repeated integration by parts

    For finite retained arrays, sum the decay bound over the polynomial number of entries counted
after Definition 6.3. An inserted frame identity instead uses the full position lattice; its infinite tail
is controlled by summing the same decay over lattice annuli. Fix the number of transitions, the
admissible complexity, and the positive beam-radius power first. Then enlarge the intermediate
position ranges by fixed polynomial powers, and choose the integration-by-parts order L for the
prescribed final error. Frequency labels spread by only a bounded amount at each transition, so
a fixed number of insertions and truncations has negligible total error. The polynomial norm
comparisons transfer this statement to all the norms in use. This estimates the error of a finite
approximation to an exact matrix product; it does not replace a transition by an arbitrarily truncated
set of edges.

Lemma 6.7 (Preliminary bounds). For a single-step map and each terminal bin,
                              X                    X
                         w2         |cv |2 ≤ δ 2        |dv |2 ,        K(cI ) ≤ m4+o(1) K(d).     (6.12)
                              v∈I                   v

In particular, γ ≤ 2.

Proof. The mass estimate is Lemma 6.1, since w = δ/m. For the capacity estimate, fix an output
test ellipse E, with both radii at least θ = mδ, and choose an arbitrarily small fixed η > 0. By
Lemma 6.6, the input entries contributing to this test, apart from negligible error, have velocities in
a translate of Cmη E. Their initial positions lie in another translate of the same enlarged ellipse.
Indeed, a contributing position equals its child position minus elapsed time times its velocity, up
to mη δ; the elapsed time is at most one and both radii of E are at least δ. Both translations are
allowed in the definition of K(d).
    Restrict the input to this one product of two translated tests. Its squared sum is at most

                               δ −2 K(d)W (Cmη E) ≤ Cm2η δ −2 K(d)W (E).

Apply the global squared operator norm bound m2 to this restricted input, and then multiply by
the output factor θ2 /W (E). Since θ2 = m2 δ 2 , the result is Cm4+2η K(d), with negligible error. The
estimate is uniform over translations, orientations, and eccentricities. Tail errors are uniform too:
θ2 /W (E) ≤ 1, and K(d) ≥ ∥d∥2∞ . Let η be arbitrarily small to obtain the stated exponent bound.
    Finally, A(cI ) ≤ G(cI ). Multiply the mass bound by the uniform upper bound for K(cI )1/2 and
sum in time with weight r. There are r−1 bins, giving A(c)3 ≤ m2+o(1) G(d)3 .

6.4   A fourth-power estimate from decoupling
We use the ordinary ℓ2 decoupling theorem of Bourgain and Demeter [4, arXiv:1403.5335v3,
Theorem 1.1, equation (2)]. For a smooth elliptic graph, Fourier thickness S −1 , and graph caps of
diameter S −1/2 , its critical three-dimensional estimate is
                                         X                          X                1/2
                                               Fτ       ≤ Cϵ S ϵ            ∥Fτ ∥24          .
                                           τ        4                   τ

The norms here are on all of spacetime. Uniformity over the normalized phases below follows from
the proof for general surfaces in [4, Section 7]: the ellipticity bounds are fixed, and the common
third-derivative bound controls the Taylor approximation on each rescaled cap.
    We derive the packet refinement for the precise class used here. The induction follows Guth–
Iosevich–Ou–Wang [12, arXiv:1808.09346v1, proof of Theorem 4.2]; compare the weighted extension
formulation in [47, arXiv:2411.08871v3, Section 4, Theorem 4.4]. Guth–Iosevich–Ou–Wang also
record an independent discovery of the refinement by Du and Zhang. The proof below specifies
which tubes count packet activity, including at the smaller induction scale. The distinction between
a packet’s activity region and its counted tube is substantive; a planar counterexample to a
support/counting mismatch is given in [36, arXiv:2608.28711v1, Proposition 2.1].

Remark 6.10 (Weighted packet costs). In the normalization of the preceding proof, the canonical
packets also have the weighted norms appearing in comparisons with refined decoupling formulations.
Write Pv = ψ(T /R)Pev as above. For a spatial box B used there, with center XB , put qB = (XB , 0)
and
                      wB (q) ≍ (1 + |q − qB |/R)−K0 ,  q = (X, T ), K0 > 4.
For every packet retained in this box,

                                       ∥Pev ∥4L4 (wB ) ≍ |dv |4 R2 ≍ ∥Pv ∥44 .

Indeed, the frequency-integral bound and Plancherel give ∥Pev (·, T )∥∞ ≲ |dv | and ∥Pev (·, T )∥22 ≲
|dv |2 R. Integration with the temporal decay of wB or ψ(T /R) proves the upper bounds. For
sufficiently small fixed c > 0, the nonnegative profile gives |Pev (X, T )| ≳ |dv | on
                                                                          √
                           0 ≤ T ≤ cR,      |X − Zv + T ∇Φ+ (ζv )| ≤ c R.

This is the short-core argument in the proof of Theorem 6.8. The region has volume comparable to
R2 . Both |ψ(T /R)| and wB are bounded below there: the time-zero centers of the retained packets
lie within distance O(R) of B, even if a tube meets the box only at a later time. This proves the
lower bounds.
Remark 6.11 (Vanishing adjacency powers). Later there will be an outer index i, a fixed number
J of transitions, and positive adjacency powers ηi → 0. For each fixed i the scale ratios satisfy
ℓj = m1/J+o(1) , so R = ℓ2j = m2/J+o(1) . Choose the external error power tending to zero with i,

                                                           79
and its localization power δi still smaller, with 2δi /J < ηi /10. Choose the sampling power less
than min(ηi , 1/J)/10. The cube comparison above then uses precisely the radius mηi w− , while the
coloring and decoupling losses are moi (1) . Here oi (1) denotes an exponent whose limiting upper
absolute value as m → ∞, with i and J fixed, tends to zero as i → ∞. We take the limits in that
order. The phase, profile, complexity, and positive radius powers remain fixed in each inner limit.
Constants may depend on i; since J is fixed, the same convention applies to the product of all
transition losses.


7     Extremal selection and propagation
We prove Theorem 6.4. We use the packet scales, lattice norms, and propagation exponent introduced
in Section 6. In particular, the amplification of a diagram with initial array d and terminal array c is

                                                A(c)3
                                                      .
                                                G(d)3

Every mask below is a coordinate projection on a layer of the diagram. Between successive masks
we retain the entire packet transition matrix. Graphs will record its significant interactions, but will
not replace the matrix by an edgewise truncation.
    We argue by contradiction and assume γ > 10κ. We use two successive extrema. The first
determines the infimum of the time-support exponents that allow amplification arbitrarily close
to γ. The second minimizes, up to a vanishing error, the asymptotic exponent of the path count
per original root among diagrams with nearly this time support. This makes the path count stable
under the later masks that retain near-extremal amplification, and supplies the counting law needed
for geometric propagation.

7.1   The least time dimension of a near-extremal family
For 0 ≤ σ ≤ 1, define γσ by the same supremum of limiting amplification exponents as γ, with the
following additional restriction. There is a fixed η > 0 within the family such that each active initial
index touches terminal masked indices in at most m2σ+o(1) time bins, where a touch means

                           |νb − νv | ≤ mη θ,     |zb − zv − tIb Uνv | ≤ mη δ.

Here the initial time is zero. The supremum still ranges over all fixed positive η and all finite
polynomial parameter bounds.

Lemma 7.1 (Minimal time dimension). One has

                           γσ ≤ 2σ,       s := inf{σ ∈ [0, 1] : γσ = γ} > 0.                       (7.1)

For every fixed σ < s, the difference γ − γσ is positive. For every σ > s in [0, 1], one has γσ = γ.
Near-extremal families defining any of these exponents may be chosen with all nonzero initial
coordinate amplitudes between 1 and 2.

Proof. Fix a family satisfying the additional support restriction. For a terminal bin I, let RI be
the initial indices touching at least one retained terminal index in I. Every matrix entry from an
initial index outside RI to a retained index in I is a locality tail. Lemma 6.6 and the polynomial
coordinate count show that replacing the input by d|RI changes the output in I by a negligible error.


                                                  80
Apply the squared-sum contraction of Lemma 6.7 to the full transition matrix on this restricted
input. Summing over I counts each initial index at most m2σ+o(1) times. Therefore

                                         |cb |2 ≤ m−2(1−σ)+o(1) δ 2
                                     X                                X
                               rw2                                        |dv |2 .
                                     b                                v


The same lemma gives K(cI ) ≤ m4+o(1) K(d) in every bin. Since A(cI ) ≤ G(cI ), multiplication by
the square root of this capacity bound proves amplification at most m2σ+o(1) .
     The classes of admissible families increase with σ. Consequently γσ is nondecreasing and at
most γ; also γ1 = γ, since there are only m2 terminal time bins. The set on which equality holds is
therefore an upper interval, possibly without its lower endpoint. Its infimum satisfies s ≥ γ/2 > 0,
and the other assertions about γσ follow. Equality at σ = s is not needed.
     To obtain comparable initial amplitudes, first normalize the largest one. The polynomial
coordinate counts and the polynomial comparison between coordinate norms and the packet norms
allow amplitudes below a sufficiently small fixed negative power of m to be discarded. The remaining
amplitudes lie in O(log m) dyadic classes. Writing the input as their sum and using linearity followed
by the triangle inequality for the output norm, one class retains at least an inverse-logarithmic
fraction of the output norm. Restriction to that class can only decrease the initial raw gauge. Its
cubed amplification therefore loses a subpower factor. Rescaling its amplitudes gives the interval
[1, 2]. The support restriction is hereditary under this operation.

    An upper bound with exponent γσ also holds with the initial atomic norm in place of the raw
gauge: decompose the input into atoms on its given support and apply the triangle inequality. In
particular, Lemma 6.5 permits this improved bound at one transition of a masked diagram whenever
the two supports of that transition satisfy the time restriction. We will repeatedly use the following
consequence:

                 a fixed improvement below the time exponent s at one transition
                                                                                                 (7.2)
                        gives a fixed improvement below γ for the diagram.

The improvement in (7.2) is measured in m-power units when the transition ratio is a fixed positive
power of m.

7.2   A second extremum: the number of paths
Fix J ≥ 1, and let mj , 0 ≤ j ≤ J, be dyadic roundings of mj/J , with m0 = 1 and mJ = m. Put
                            mj+1
                     ℓj =        ,       θj = mj δ,        rj = m−2
                                                                 j ,           wj = rj θj .
                             mj

Thus logm ℓj → 1/J. Insert the intermediate frame identities and truncate to polynomial grids, as
permitted by Lemma 6.6. A diagram consists of these exact transitions and arbitrary vertex masks,
including a possible initial mask. Its input array d is normalized as in Lemma 7.1, on a root set
R; amplification is always measured against the entire array on this set, even if an additional root
mask is used.
    For each fixed J, an index i will label a sequence of families. Within family i, the polynomial
bounds of Definition 6.3 and all positive radius powers are fixed before m → ∞; these bounds may
depend on i. We write oi (1) in an exponent for an error whose limiting upper absolute value as
m → ∞ tends to zero as i → ∞. This includes subpower losses within a fixed family. Thus J
remains fixed while we first take the inner limit in m and then the outer limit in i. The construction


                                                      81
is valid for every fixed J; we will later choose one sufficiently large for the geometric time-profile
hypothesis.
    For a fixed positive radius power η, join an active index v at level j to an active index b at level
j + 1 when

               Ib ⊂ Iv ,    |νb − νv | ≤ mη θj+1 ,          |zb − zv − (tIb − tIv )Uνv | ≤ mη wj .   (7.3)

A path is a sequence of such edges from an active root to a terminal leaf.

Locality for error diagrams. We will repeatedly discard a vertex class whose contribution
has a nonadjacent edge in every complete matrix-product summand. For every prescribed A > 0,
this contribution is O(m−A maxv∈R |dv |), both coordinatewise and in the packet norms. Indeed,
Lemma 6.6 makes the nonadjacent coefficient smaller than any prescribed negative power; the
remaining coefficients and the number of summands have polynomial bounds, and there are only J
layers. Choose the locality decay order after the graph radius and these fixed family bounds. This
applies, in particular, to roots with no graph path to the tested output and to intermediate vertices
with no suffix to that output. It bounds the error of the vertex masking; the retained diagram
continues to use the full transition matrices.

Lemma 7.2 (Endpoint localization). Along a path from level a to level b > a, the endpoint angle
difference is OJ (mη θb ), and

                                |zb − za − (tIb − tIa )Uνa | ≤ OJ (mη wa ).

For a fixed root and a fixed terminal leaf, the number of paths is at most mO(Jη)+o(1) .

Proof. The angular differences are bounded by a geometric sum of the successive child widths. For
the position bound, write tj = tIj , Uj = Uνj , and ej = zj+1 − zj − (tj+1 − tj )Uj . Telescoping gives

                                                 b−1
                                                 X             b−1
                                                               X
                      zb − za − (tb − ta )Ua =         ej +          (tb − tj )(Uj − Uj−1 ).
                                                 j=a          j=a+1

The edge errors satisfy |ej | ≤ mη wj . For a switch from level j − 1 to level j, Lipschitz continuity of
the velocity map gives |Uj − Uj−1 | ≲ mη θj , while nesting gives 0 ≤ tb − tj ≤ rj . Its contribution
is therefore O(mη θj rj ) = O(mη wj ). Summing and using wj ≤ wa proves the positional endpoint
bound.
    For fixed endpoints, the intermediate time interval at each level is the unique ancestor of the
terminal interval. At level j, the angular grid has spacing θj , while the angle lies within OJ (mη θj )
of the root angle. There are mO(η)+o(1) choices. Given this angle, the endpoint bound applied from
level j to the terminal leaf locates zj within OJ (mη wj ) of a prescribed point. On the position grid of
spacing comparable to wj , this again gives mO(η)+o(1) choices. Multiplication over the fixed number
of intermediate levels proves the claim.

   At accuracy ϵ > 0, call a family of diagrams admissible if it has a fixed radius power 0 < η < ϵ,
amplification at least mγ−ϵ , and at most m2(s+ϵ) terminal time bins accessible from every root by
paths at that radius. For such a family consider the limiting lower exponent of

                                                 #{paths}
                                                          .                                          (7.4)
                                                   #R


                                                       82
Lemma 7.3 (Minimal path count). Admissible families exist at arbitrarily small accuracies. The
infima of the limiting lower exponents in (7.4) have a finite limit as ϵ ↓ 0. There are families
approaching this limit, with accuracies tending to zero, for which the following holds. Call their
graphs the high graphs. Choose a smaller fixed positive radius power in each family, tending to zero
with its accuracy, and call the corresponding graphs low graphs. After any fixed number of further
vertex maskings that retain amplification mγ−oi (1) , one has

                     #{remaining low paths} ≥ m−oi (1) #{original high paths}.                       (7.5)

Proof. If s < 1, choose a support exponent strictly between s and s + ϵ; its propagation exponent
is γ by Lemma 7.1. If s = 1, use the unrestricted families. In either case choose a near-extremal
single-step family with the desired time sparsity at some fixed enlarged direct radius, and make
its input amplitudes comparable. Insert the intermediate identities. By Lemma 7.2, a sufficiently
smaller fixed radius power at the intermediate levels ensures that every path endpoint is a direct
touch at the originally chosen radius. Truncation errors and dyadic losses can be made smaller than
the unused accuracy. This gives the required admissible families.
    At each step, a parent has at most O(m4η ℓ2j ) children in a given time bin: the angular grid
contributes O(m2η ) choices and the position grid contributes O(m2η ℓ2j ). There are ℓ2j child time
bins. Hence the total path count per root is at most m4+4Jη+o(1) . For a lower bound, let R′ be
the roots having at least one path. All other roots contribute a negligible error by the locality
observation above. Since 1 ≤ |dv | ≤ 2,
                                                         #R′
                                       G(d|R′ )3 ≤ C         G(d)3 .
                                                         #R
Here the squared mass drops in the displayed proportion, and its capacity does not increase.
Composition with exponent γ + o(1), together with the lower amplification, gives #R′ /#R ≥
m−ϵ−o(1) . There is at least one path per root of R′ , proving a lower bound for the exponent in (7.4).
The admissible classes are nested as ϵ decreases, so their infima have a finite monotone limit; denote
it by ΛJ .
    Choose near-minimizers with accuracies ϵi ↓ 0, and pass to subsequences on which their path-
count exponents approach ΛJ also from above. Let their fixed high radius powers be ηi , and choose
low powers 0 < ηilo ≪ ηi . Suppose further masks have amplification mγ−ρi +o(1) with ρi → 0. The
resulting low graph inherits the original upper bound on accessible terminal bins. It is therefore
an admissible competitor with accuracy tending to zero, for example an accuracy slightly larger
than max(ϵi , ρi , ηilo ). Its limiting lower exponent is at least ΛJ − oi (1). The original high graph has
exponent at most ΛJ + oi (1) on the chosen subsequence. This proves (7.5). All adaptive masks still
form families with fixed polynomial bounds at each i, so this comparison applies to each of a fixed
finite number of such selections.

   Figure 2 shows how this path-retention property enters the rest of the proof.
   Fix these high and low radii. Between them choose a fixed intermediate radius power ηimid in
each family, so that ηilo < ηimid < ηi . The strict gaps absorb bounded constants in neighborhood
containments once m is sufficiently large. Figure 3 illustrates the inclusions used below.

Lemma 7.5 (Incoming counts and terminal selection). Further vertex masks can be chosen retaining

7.4   The conditional time law
The masks now control prefix counts and incoming leaf degrees while preserving near-extremal
amplification. To apply geometric propagation, we still need both support counts and maximal
probabilities at every time depth, conditional on each retained root. We obtain these from uniform
counting on a selected set of paths. This counting law is auxiliary; the coefficients continue to come
from the exact masked matrix maps.
   All graph statistics Dj and Sj still refer to the original high graph. All low path counts in this
subsection refer to the final masks of Lemma 7.5.
Lemma 7.6 (A linear conditional time profile). There is a subset Ω of the final low paths occupying
a fraction m−oi (1) of them such that uniform counting on Ω has these properties:
  (i) Its root marginal is comparable to the uniform law on a set of N0 roots.
 (ii) Given each root, the terminal time satisfies (2.1), with base M = m2 , target exponent s, and
      error O(1/J) + oi (1).
The subset is used only to define this law; it does not change any internal summand of the terminal
coefficients.
Proof. Let H be the original high path count and L the final low path count. By Lemma 7.3,
L ≥ m−αi H, where αi = oi (1). Write T (j) for the level-j time interval containing the terminal time.
For a vertex v, let PH (v) and PL (v) be its high and final low prefix counts, and let DL (v) be its
final low suffix count. Thus PL (v) ≤ PH (v) and DL (v) ≤ Dj (v).
    Choose βi ↓ 0 sufficiently slowly that βi − αi dominates the other errors and mαi −βi → 0 within
each family. Call a remaining level-j vertex bad if

                                             DL (v) < m−βi Dj (v).

The number of final low paths through bad vertices at this level is bounded by

                                PL (v)DL (v) ≤ m−βi             PH (v)Dj (v) = m−βi H.
                         X                                X

                        v bad                               v


                                                         87
In the final sum, v ranges over all vertices of the original high graph at that level. Its equality to H
holds because every high path visits exactly one such vertex. Summing over J + 1 levels shows that
the event G that every visited vertex is good has probability 1 − o(1) under uniform final low path
counting.
    Consider this unrestricted uniform low law, conditional on a complete vertex prefix ending at a
good v at level j. For a prescribed child time bin I, its next-bin probability is
                                X
                                             DL (b)
                          b low child of v
                               Ib =I                                  s′j
                                                      ≤ mβi +oi (1)       ≤ moi (1) ℓj−2s .        (7.8)
                                  DL (v)                              dj
If the numerator is nonzero, choose one remaining child b in that bin. Its intermediate neighborhood
contains every other low child there, and the original high suffix sum on the neighborhood is
comparable to s′j . This bounds the numerator. Goodness and Dj (v) ≍ dj bound the denominator,
and (7.6) gives the final inequality.
    To iterate (7.8), kill a path as soon as it visits a bad vertex. Fix a nested sequence of time bins
through level j. Conditional on each surviving vertex prefix, the next prescribed-bin probability is
bounded by (7.8). Averaging over the vertex prefixes having the same time prefix preserves that
bound. Induction, with the process killed at every step, therefore gives, for every root v and level-j
bin I,
                                                            ℓ−2s = moi (1) m−2s
                                                          Y
                           Pr(G, T (j) = I | v) ≤ moi (1)    h              j .                   (7.9)
                                                              h<j
If the root is bad the left side is zero. In writing G on the left, one may first require survival only
through level j, for which the induction applies directly, and then impose survival at later levels.
This can only decrease the probability. Multiple vertex prefixes with the same time prefix introduce
no additional count.
    Discard roots for which Pr(G | v) < 1/2. Their total probability under the original low root
law is at most 2 Pr(G c ) = o(1). Keep the good paths on the other roots. Dividing (7.9) by their
conditional survival probability costs at most two, so the maximum probability of a level-j time bin,
conditional on each retained root, is m−2s logm mj +oi (1) . The support bound at that level is (7.7).
    Between consecutive level depths, monotonicity of probabilities uses the preceding level for the
maximum weight, and monotonicity of supports uses the following level for the number of occupied
bins. Since the gap in base-M = m2 depths is 1/J + o(1), these give (2.1) at every dyadic depth
with error at most s/J + oi (1). The dyadic roundings change only bounded factors.
    The retained measure is still uniform on a subset of paths. Its positive root weights have
polynomial ratios, because the numbers of paths are positive integers with polynomial upper bounds.
Select a dyadic class of root weights carrying an inverse-logarithmic fraction of the measure, and
keep all retained paths from those roots. If their number is N0 , the new root weights are comparable
to 1/N0 . This selection of whole root fibers does not change the root-conditional time laws and
retains a fraction m−oi (1) of all final low paths.

7.5   Geometry bounds the capacity of the output
Fix a positive ζ < γ − 10κ. Choose J sufficiently large that the O(1/J) error in Lemma 7.6 lies
within the small-error range of Theorem 2.2 for exponent 1 + 2s − 10κ − ζ/2, and then take i
sufficiently large. All the preceding selections are made for this fixed J.
    For a subset P of the final low paths, say that a root v is represented in a terminal bin I if some
path of P starts at v and ends in I. Count each such root once in that bin, regardless of the number
of its paths. Call the paths in P represented paths.

                                                         88
Lemma 7.7 (Represented roots). Fix any polynomial upper bound on the radii within each family.
There is a subset P, which may depend on this bound, occupying a fraction m−oi (1) of all final low
paths with the following property. In every terminal bin I, the number of represented roots satisfying

                                Uνv ∈ u + F,        zv + tI Uνv ∈ x + rF

is at most
                      K(d)δ −2 W (F ) m−2(1+s−10κ−ζ)+oi (1) ,      radii(F ) ≥ m2 δ,                (7.10)
uniformly over all independent centers u, x ∈ R2 and all radii up to the chosen upper bound. One can
keep a fraction m−oi (1) of the final leaves such that every kept leaf has at least km−oi (1) represented
incoming paths.

Proof. Put the uniform law of Lemma 7.6 on its path subset, take the root as base label, and assign
it line parameters (Uνv , zv ). The root weights are comparable to 1/N0 , and 1 ≤ |dv | ≤ 2. The input
capacity definition therefore gives the density constant

                                                    K(d)δ −2
                                             K0 ≲
                                                      N0
for tests with radii at least δ.
    We fix the width range before applying geometry. Choose a polynomial cutoff H ≥ m2 δ bounding
the norms of all tested velocities and all spatial coordinates after division by r, including the root-line
positions and terminal position labels. In the axes of a rectangular test with half-widths ej , each
coordinate interval meets the bounded support in a set contained in an interval of half-width
min(ej , H). Choose the two centers independently, using the same clipped half-widths and axes.
The resulting joint test covers the original test on the support, and its weight is no larger, up to
the fixed ellipse–rectangle comparison. Thus one polynomial width range controls the full terminal
capacity supremum.
    Choose B = mA larger than this cutoff, the upper width bound in the statement, and the root
coordinate bounds, with a fixed power margin. This margin also covers the endpoint enlargements.
With M = m2 , we have B ≥ M δ. Rescale both line coordinates by B −1 . The new input floor is

                         δ ′ = δ/B = M −Dm ,        Dm = 12 (logm N + A) ≥ 1.

The density constant becomes K0 B 2 , since W is homogeneous of degree two. The output weight of
a test is W (F/B) = B −2 W (F ), so these factors cancel on returning to the old coordinates. The
new output floor M δ ′ corresponds exactly to M δ in the old coordinates. This also covers m = N ,
for which the unnormalized floor would have given the inadmissible value D = 1/2. To make the
complexity fixed, take a subsequence with Dm → D0 , and choose D∗ = D0 + a, where 0 < a < ζ/16.
Eventually 0 < D∗ − Dm < 2a. A radius at least M −D∗ but below the available input floor M −Dm
can therefore be rounded up by a factor at most M 2a . Doing this to both radii increases W by
at most M 4a = m8a . Thus the input cap at the fixed complexity D∗ costs at most this additional
factor. The output floor for D∗ is smaller than the available output floor, so every required output
test is still allowed.
    The definition of the geometric exponent as a small-error limit of an infimum over finite
complexities is used here: the sufficiently small time-error threshold may be chosen for all finite D∗ ,
although the large-base threshold and subpower losses can depend on that fixed complexity. Thus
the preceding choice of J, followed by i and then the family base, is valid.



                                                    89
    Apply Theorem 2.2, with base M = m2 , to obtain a dominated sublaw of subpower mass. The
difference between the chosen geometric exponent and 1 + 2s − 10κ − ζ absorbs the arbitrarily small
fixed rounding loss just described. After normalization, its line-test probabilities satisfy

                                                 K(d)δ −2
                                                          W (F ) m−2(1+2s−10κ−ζ)+oi (1) .
                                             
               Pr T1 = t, U ∈ F, Xt ∈ rF ≤                                                         (7.11)

As throughout, translations in the two tests are independent.
    There are at most N0 m2s+oi (1) possible root–terminal-time pairs. Discard pairs whose probability
in this normalized law is less than
                                             N0−1 m−2s−τi ,
where τi = oi (1) tends to zero slowly enough to dominate the support error. This discards o(1)
mass. Take P to be the path atoms in the support of the law after this deletion. Each represented
root in a specified terminal bin has pair probability at least this threshold. Dividing (7.11) by the
threshold proves (7.10), with all normalization factors absorbed in moi (1) .
    Let L again be the final low path count. The starting uniform law for geometry is on a subset of at
least m−oi (1) L paths. Its dominated geometric sublaw retains subpower mass. After normalization,
the weight of any path atom is consequently at most moi (1) /L. The remaining support therefore
represents at least m−oi (1) L paths, even though their new weights need not be equal.
    Every original final leaf has between k and 2J k incoming low paths. Choose a threshold km−υi ,
with υi = oi (1) sufficiently slow, and keep the leaves with at least this many represented incoming
paths. The represented paths going to the other leaves are at most km−υi times the original leaf
count, which is negligible compared with the total represented path count. Since no leaf has more
than 2J k incoming paths, the kept leaves occupy a fraction m−oi (1) of all final leaves. Lemma 7.5
ensures that this last output restriction retains amplification mγ−oi (1) .

contradiction. Thus the assumption γ > 10κ is impossible, which proves the propagation bound
and its uniform finite-family formulation.


8     Passage to a global estimate
The packet theorem first gives a cubic estimate on widely separated balls, with an arbitrarily small
power loss. We remove that loss by covering a finite list of peaks with sparse families of balls. The
initial finiteness of the peak list follows from an elementary fourth-power estimate for the original
sphere measure.
    The choices have a fixed order: first the separation exponent C0 , then the desired distributional
error α, and finally the power loss ϵ. The covering radii may depend on α; the sparse estimate must
therefore permit ϵ to be chosen after those radii have been bounded.
    This passage from local estimates to a global bound follows the sparse-family strategy of Tao [38];
compare the bounded-data formulation in [5, arXiv:1012.3760v3, Appendix, Lemmas A1–A2]. We
prove the required covering and cubic estimates below.

8.1   A sparse estimate for graph patches
Normalize first by ∥g∥L∞ (S 2 ,σ) = 1. Partition the sphere into finitely many measurable pieces
contained in the graph patches of Section 6. Subdivide these pieces, if necessary, so that on each
piece one of the terminal windows χν10 is bounded below by a positive constant. Such a subdivision
exists because the windows form a square partition of unity with bounded overlap. In the rotated
coordinates of one piece, write its extension as
                                    Z
             F0 (y) = F0 (x, t) =          f (ξ)χν10 (ξ)e2πi(x·ξ+tϕ(ξ)) dξ,   ∥f ∥∞ + ∥f ∥2 ≤ C.   (8.1)
                                     R2

Here f is supported in a fixed compact frequency patch. The smooth surface-area density and
division by χν10 have been absorbed into f . All constants in this section may depend on these finitely
many fixed choices, but are independent of the original bounded measurable data. In particular, no
differentiability of f is assumed.

8.2    A finite list of peaks
We now use the geometry of the original sphere to obtain a global estimate at a larger exponent.
The convolution calculation keeps the normalization of surface measure explicit and applies directly
to bounded measurable complex data.

Lemma 8.2 (An elementary fourth-power bound). For every bounded measurable h : S 2 → C,

                                          ∥Eh∥4L4 (R3 ) ≤ 32π 3 ∥h∥4∞ .                                (8.9)

Moreover Eh and its first derivatives are uniformly bounded by constants times ∥h∥∞ .

Proof. The convolution of surface measure with itself has density
                                                           2π
                                         (σ ∗ σ)(y) =         1          .                            (8.10)

second variable, s = ω · ν has density 2π on [−1, 1], and |ω + ν| = (2 + 2s)1/2 . Thus, for a radial
test function q,               Z                          Z              2

                                                         4π 2

                                                          95
Let λ = hσ, a finite complex measure. Its convolution satisfies the total-variation domination

                                       |λ ∗ λ| ≤ ∥h∥2∞ (σ ∗ σ),

so it has an L2 density of squared norm at most 32π 3 ∥h∥4∞ . The function (Eh)2 is the Fourier
transform, with the positive phase convention, of this convolution. Plancherel proves (8.9). Finally,
differentiating the defining integral is justified by boundedness of the frequency support and gives,
for example,
                            ∥Eh∥∞ ≤ 4π∥h∥∞ ,          ∥∇Eh∥∞ ≤ 8π 2 ∥h∥∞ .


   Each function F0 in (8.1), in its rotated coordinates, is the extension of a uniformly bounded
density supported on one of the original sphere pieces. Lemma 8.2 therefore supplies uniform L4 ,
L∞ , and Lipschitz bounds for F0 .
Lemma 8.3 (Initial peak count). For 0 < µ ≤ 1, the number of unit lattice cubes Q′ satisfying
supQ′ |F0 | ≥ µ is at most Cµ−7 . One may select a point xQ′ ∈ Q′ in each such cube with |F0 (xQ′ )| ≥
µ/2.
Proof. Take the unit cubes to be half-open, and select such a point in every cube of an arbitrary
finite subcollection. A uniform Lipschitz bound shows that |F0 | ≥ µ/4 on the ball of radius cµ
about each selected point, where c > 0 is fixed and small. Each ball contributes at least c′ µ7 to the
integral of |F0 |4 . These balls have bounded overlap, since their radii are bounded and there is one
center in each of distinct unit lattice cubes. Lemma 8.2 bounds the size of the finite subcollection
by Cµ−7 . Since the same bound holds for every finite subcollection, the whole set of such cubes is
finite and has the asserted size.

8.3   Sparse covering and reproduction
The covering lemma converts the initial polynomial peak count into sparse families whose number is
an arbitrarily small power of µ−1 . Their radii remain polynomial in µ−1 , with an exponent fixed
once α is chosen. This is the information needed to apply the sparse cubic estimate and then choose
its loss.
Lemma 8.4 (Sparse covering). Fix C0 > 1 and A > 0. For every 0 < α < 1 there is a constant
Cα with the following property. If µ > 0 is sufficiently small and X is a finite indexed list of at
most Aµ−7 points in R3 , then X is covered by at most Cα µ−α/2 families of balls. Each family has a
common radius ρ with
                                    µ−1 ≤ ρ,      2ρ ≤ µ−Cα ,                                (8.11)
and its distinct centers are separated by at least (2ρ/µ)C0 . Each family has at most #X balls.
Proof. Put β = α/4, choose an integer J > 8/β, and set D = 2C0 + 2. For sufficiently small µ, we
have #X ≤ µ−8 . Define

          R0 = µ−1 ,      Rj+1 = (Rj /µ)D
                                                                                          
                                              (0 ≤ j < J),        nj (x) = # X ∩ B(x, Rj ) ,

where balls are open and counts include the multiplicities of the indexed list. For every point x of
the list, some j < J satisfies
                                      nj+1 (x) ≤ µ−β nj (x).                                  (8.12)
Otherwise nJ (x) > µ−Jβ n0 (x) ≥ µ−Jβ would contradict #X ≤ µ−8 .

                                                 96
    Assign each point one such index j, and group the points further according to 2k ≤ nj (x) < 2k+1 .
There are Oα (log(1/µ)) groups. In each group choose a maximal disjoint subcollection of the
radius-Rj balls centered at its points. The triples of the selected balls cover that group.
    Join two selected centers by an edge when their distance is less than Rj+1 /2. For a selected
center a, the radius-Rj balls centered at its neighbors are disjoint and lie in B(a, Rj+1 ), since
Rj ≤ Rj+1 /2. Each such ball captures at least 2k indices of the whole list, whereas (8.12) gives
nj+1 (a) < 2k+1 µ−β . The graph therefore has degree at most 2µ−β . Greedy coloring splits the
selected balls into O(µ−β ) colors. Including all the groups, the number of families is at most

                                    Cα µ−β log(1/µ) ≤ Cα µ−α/2 .

   Use radius ρ = 3Rj for each covering family. Centers in one color have distance at least Rj+1 /2,
and, for sufficiently small µ,
                          Rj+1  1
                               = (Rj /µ)2C0 +2 ≥ (6Rj /µ)C0 = (2ρ/µ)C0 .
                           2    2
Finally, write Rj = µ−aj , so that a0 = 1 and aj+1 = D(aj + 1). The finite number J depends only
on α and C0 . Enlarging Cα to at least aJ−1 + 1 gives 6Rj ≤ µ−Cα for small µ, which proves (8.11).
The selected balls are centered at points of the list, so every family has at most #X balls.

Proposition 8.5 (Distributional bound). For every 0 < α < 1 and all sufficiently small µ > 0,

                                |{y ∈ R3 : |F0 (y)| > µ}| ≤ Cα µ−3−α .                         (8.13)

Proof. Let X = (xQ′ ) be the finite list supplied by Lemma 8.3. It suffices to prove #X ≤ Cα µ−3−α ,
since the level set in (8.13) is contained in the corresponding unit cubes.
    The Fourier transform of F0 , as a tempered distribution, is supported in a fixed compact set.
Choose a Schwartz function ψ whose Fourier transform is 1 on a neighborhood of that set. Then
F0 = ψ ∗ F0 ; the convolution is absolutely convergent and the identity holds pointwise because F0 is
bounded and continuous. Hölder’s inequality yields

                                                   R3

                                                B∈F   2B

For the last inequality, the doubled balls satisfy all the hypotheses of Lemma 8.1: their radius is
at least µ−1 , their separation is the required one, and their number is at most #X ≤ µ−8 after
reducing the threshold for µ.
    Fix α first, and then choose ϵ > 0 so small that Cα ϵ ≤ α/2, using the exponent in (8.11). Thus

                                              µ3 #XF ≤ Cα µ−α/2 .

There are at most Cα µ−α/2 families. Each index was assigned exactly once, and summing proves
#X ≤ Cα µ−3−α , as required.

Proof of Theorem 1.1. Fix p > 3 and choose 0 < α < min{1, p − 3}. Let µ∗ > 0 be smaller than
the thresholds required above for this choice of α. By the layer-cake identity and Proposition 8.5,
the contribution of sufficiently small thresholds is bounded by
                           Z µ∗                                    Z µ∗
                                  p−1
                       p          µ     |{|F0 | > µ}| dµ ≤ Cp,α             µp−4−α dµ < ∞.
                            0                                           0

For larger thresholds, the uniform L4 estimate gives |{|F0 | > µ}| ≤ Cµ−4 , and the level set is empty
above the uniform L∞ bound. Their contribution is therefore finite as well. We conclude that
∥F0 ∥p ≤ Cp .
   There are only finitely many graph pieces in (8.1). Rotations preserve Lebesgue measure, so
the Lp triangle inequality gives ∥Eg∥p ≤ Cp under the normalization ∥g∥∞ = 1. Multiplication by
∥g∥∞ proves the result for arbitrary nonzero bounded measurable complex data. When the essential
supremum is zero, the extension is identically zero. All constructions and estimates depend only on
the almost-everywhere class of the data, which completes the assertion of the theorem.


9     Spherical consequences
The global sphere estimate has now been proved. We give the complete transfer to the translated-
tube maximal operator defined in the introduction, retaining the weighted tube estimate needed for
its strong operator norm. This uses sphere factorization in addition to Theorem 1.1.

9.1   The Kakeya maximal transfer
Proof of Corollary 1.3. Sphere factorization, due to Bourgain [2], upgrades Theorem 1.1 to
∥Eg∥Lq (R3 ) ≲q ∥g∥Lq (S 2 ) for every q > 3; see [6, Section 1.1, (1.1)–(1.2)] for the weak-type
factorization and interpolation in the sphere case. Fix 3 < q < 4 and put r = q/2. Consider unit
tubes Tj = Tδ (xj , ωj ) in δ-separated directions. Choose smooth nonnegative bumps bj , bounded by
1, supported on disjoint caps of radius cδ about ωj , and equal to 1 on the concentric caps of radius
cδ/2. Here c > 0 is a fixed small constant. The modulation
                                                              −2 x ·ζ
                                            hj (ζ) = e−2πiδ       j
                                                                        bj (ζ)

translates the extension to the center of δ −2 Tj . We have ∥hj ∥qq ≲ δ 2 and |Ehj | ≳ δ 2 on that dilated
tube. To see the lower bound, write y − δ −2 xj = sωj + v, where |s| ≤ δ −2 /2, v ⊥ ωj , and |v| ≤ δ −1 .

                                                       98
On the cap, the phase after removing its value at ωj has magnitude at most C(c + c2 ). Taking
c sufficiently small makes the real part of the remaining integrand a fixed positive fraction of bj ,
whose integral is comparable to δ 2 .
    For arbitrary weights aj ≥ 0 and independent random signs εj , Khintchine’s inequality and the
diagonal estimate give
                               X                 r             X
                                                                        1/2
                                                                                 q                  X
                      δ 2q−6            aj 1Tj       ≲q E E          εj aj hj           ≲q δ 2           arj .
                                                 r                                  q
                                j                                j                                   j

The factor δ −6 comes from y = δ −2 x, while the last inequality uses the disjoint cap supports. Taking
the rth root gives the weighted tube estimate
                                X                                            X           1/r
                                         aj 1Tj             ≲q δ 12/q−4 δ 2         arj          .
                                                     Lr (R )
                                                          3
                                    j                                           j

    Set p = r′ = q/(q − 2). For nonnegative f ∈ Lp (R3 ) and cj ≥ 0, the normalization |Tj | = πδ 2
gives
                           1            1                               X 1/r
                    X           Z         Z X
                 δ2    cj            f=    f   cj 1Tj ≲q δ 12/q−4 ∥f ∥p δ 2   crj .
                     j
                          |Tj |  T j    π    j                              j

Duality for the discrete measure assigning mass δ 2 to each direction bounds the p-norm of these tube
averages. The bound is uniform in all tube locations, so each Tj may independently approximate
the supremum defining Kδ f (ωj ).
    Choose a maximal δ-separated net on S 2 , with angular cells of area comparable to δ 2 . Every
direction in a cell is within δ of its center ωj . For δ < 1/4, the corresponding tubes satisfy

                          Tδ (a, ω) ⊂ T2δ (a − ωj /4, ωj ) ∪ T2δ (a + ωj /4, ωj ).

Indeed, the transverse displacement is at most 3δ/2 and the axial displacement at most 1/2+δ 2 < 3/4.
Thus Kδ f (ω) ≤ 8K2δ f (ωj ). Sphere packing splits the net into a fixed number of 2δ-separated
families. Apply the discrete bound at width 2δ to each family and sum over the angular cells. This
gives Bourgain’s strong maximal transfer, in the form stated in [21, Theorem 3.3, pp. 10–11]:
                                                                                             q
                          ∥Kδ f ∥Lp (S 2 ) ≲q δ 12/q−4 ∥f ∥Lp (R3 ) ,               p=          .
                                                                                            q−2

For δ ≥ 1/4, the same bound follows directly from Hölder’s inequality. Interpolating with ∥Kδ f ∥∞ ≤
∥f ∥∞ , at parameter p/3, yields

                               ∥Kδ f ∥L3 (S 2 ) ≲q δ −4(q−3)/(3(q−2)) ∥f ∥L3 (R3 ) .

Choosing q > 3 sufficiently close to 3 proves the claim.

    This deduction starts from the spherical conclusion of Theorem 1.1 and uses sphere-specific
factorization; it does not invoke the general-surface diagonal companion or the independent maximal
theorem.




                                                               99
9.2   The open mixed-norm range on the sphere
The diagonal sphere estimates also give the strict mixed-norm range. For 1 < a ≤ ∞ and 3 < b < ∞,
write a′ = a/(a − 1) when a < ∞ and a′ = 1 when a = ∞. Then

                             ∥Eg∥Lb (R3 ) ≤ Ca,b ∥g∥La (S 2 ) ,   b > 2a′ ,                    (9.1)

for every complex g ∈ La (S 2 ). The defining integral for E remains meaningful because σ(S 2 ) < ∞
implies La (S 2 ) ⊂ L1 (S 2 ). Thus the conclusion covers a larger class of input densities, with an
output exponent depending on their integrability.
    Indeed, for 1 < a ≤ b, set r = 1 + b/a′ > 3 and ϑ = r/b ≤ 1. Interpolate the diagonal
L (S 2 ) → Lr (R3 ) estimate from sphere factorization with the elementary L1 (S 2 ) → L∞ (R3 )
  r

estimate. The identities
                                      1   ϑ       1            ϑ
                                        = ,         =1−ϑ+
                                      b    r      a            r
give (9.1); when a = b, the diagonal estimate itself suffices. For b < a < ∞, use the diagonal
estimate at b and Hölder’s inequality on S 2 . The case a = ∞ is Theorem 1.1. This argument uses
the strict inequality b > 2a′ to choose r > 3 and makes no assertion on the scaling line b = 2a′ .
